Best Prompt for Business Plan: From Idea to Investor-Ready in 2026

Best Prompt for Business Plan: From Idea to Investor-Ready in 2026

June 20, 2026 · Tutorial · 10 min read

Writing a business plan used to take weeks. In 2026, the best prompt for business plan generation lets you turn a half-formed idea into a 10-page investor-ready document in an afternoon — if you know how to structure the prompt. This guide gives you the exact 7-section framework, the four reusable prompt templates, and the three mistakes that kill otherwise strong plans.

Why "best prompt for business plan" is suddenly a 2026 search trend

In 2026, business plans are no longer 50-page documents designed to impress — they are "proof that your startup deserves to exist, scale, and lead," as PrometAI puts it. Investors read between the lines: they evaluate how you think, organize, and anticipate challenges. They have seen thousands of AI-generated plans, so the bar for what passes as "investor-ready" has risen sharply.

The shift is structural. A 2026 CustomCPA study found that Canadian lenders, BDC, and major banks now expect financial projections backed by clearly stated assumptions, market analysis grounded in verifiable data, and management sections that demonstrate relevant experience. Generic output gets rejected before the second paragraph. The good news: a well-designed prompt forces you to feed in those exact inputs — TAM numbers, CAC math, founder credentials — so the AI has no choice but to produce something credible.

The 7 sections every investor-ready plan needs

Before you touch a prompt, lock the structure. The most reliable plans in 2026 follow a 7-section backbone. Memorize this — every prompt template below is just a re-arrangement of the same seven blocks.

  1. Executive Summary — 1 page, written last but placed first. Problem, solution, market size, traction, ask.
  2. Company & Problem — legal structure, mission, the specific pain you solve.
  3. Market Analysis — TAM / SAM / SOM with sources, ICP definition, market trends.
  4. Product & Differentiation — what you build, why it wins, moat.
  5. Go-to-Market & Sales — channels, pricing, CAC, LTV, sales cycle.
  6. Operations & Team — founder bios, org chart, key hires.
  7. Financial Projections — 3-year P&L, assumptions, funding ask, use of funds.

If a section is missing, the plan is incomplete. Period. Investors skim in this exact order.

Prompt #1 — The Discovery Prompt (run this FIRST)

Never start by asking ChatGPT to "write a business plan." That's the single biggest mistake founders make. The first prompt should be a structured interview — it forces you to think through the business before the AI writes anything.

Act as a senior strategy consultant from McKinsey with 15 years
helping pre-seed startups raise their first round. You will NOT
write the plan yet. Instead, interview me section by section.

For EACH of the 7 sections below, ask me 3–5 sharp questions
that a partner at Sequoia would actually ask. Do not summarize
my answers. Do not write any prose. Just ask the questions.

Sections:
1. Executive Summary
2. Company & Problem
3. Market Analysis (TAM/SAM/SOM)
4. Product & Differentiation
5. Go-to-Market (channels, CAC, LTV)
6. Operations & Team (founder bios)
7. Financial Projections (3-year)

Start with Section 1. Wait for my answers before moving on.
If my answer is vague, push back with a follow-up question.

Why this works: it converts the AI into a thinking partner instead of a copywriter. The questions surface assumptions you didn't know you were making — especially around CAC and LTV, where 70% of first-time founders hand-wave.

Prompt #2 — The 7-Section Master Prompt (the workhorse)

Once you have answered the discovery questions, paste them into this template. This is the best prompt for business plan generation in its raw form — copy it, fill the bracketed slots, and you have a first draft in under three minutes.

Act as an experienced startup CFO who has prepared 200+
business plans that successfully raised between $250K and
$10M. Write a complete 7-section business plan using the
answers below. Tone: confident, data-driven, no fluff. Length:
8–10 pages. Use bullet points where density matters. Use
short paragraphs (max 4 sentences).

== ANSWERS ==

Company name: [your name]
One-line description: [e.g. "B2B SaaS for HVAC contractors"]
Stage: [pre-idea / prototype / first-revenue / scaling]
Target customer (ICP): [e.g. "US HVAC companies with 5–50 techs"]
Problem: [2–3 sentences]
Solution: [2–3 sentences]
TAM: [$X] — source: [citation]
SAM: [$Y]
SOM (3-year reachable): [$Z]
Direct competitors: [3 names + 1-line each]
Your unfair advantage: [1 paragraph]
Pricing model: [e.g. "$200/mo per tech, $50/mo add-on"]
Estimated CAC: [$X]
Estimated LTV: [$Y]
LTV:CAC ratio target: [3:1]
Distribution channels: [paid ads / outbound / partnerships]
Founder 1: [name, role, 1-line bio, prior exit if any]
Founder 2: [name, role, 1-line bio]
Year-1 revenue target: [$X]
Year-3 revenue target: [$Y]
Funding ask: [$Z]
Use of funds: [percentages: hiring / marketing / product / runway]

Write all 7 sections. End with a 1-paragraph "Why Now?"
closing argument.

The key trick is the "Use of funds" line. Investors always ask "how will you spend this?" — and 80% of plans fumble it. By forcing percentages in the prompt, you get a clean pie chart on the first pass.

Prompt #3 — The Financial Model Deep-Dive

The single weakest section of any AI-generated plan is financials. Use this follow-up prompt after Prompt #2 returns the prose version.

Take the 3-year financial section from the plan above and
produce a structured financial model. Format as a markdown
table with these rows per year (Y1, Y2, Y3):

  - Revenue (broken into 3–5 product lines)
  - COGS
  - Gross profit & gross margin %
  - Operating expenses (split: payroll, marketing, R&D, G&A)
  - EBITDA & EBITDA margin %
  - Cash burn
  - Cash on hand (assume starting at funding-ask amount)
  - Months of runway at end of each year

Then add a "Key Assumptions" block with 8 numbered bullets
showing the math behind every line. Show your work.

Then add a "Sensitivity Analysis" section: what happens to
runway if CAC is 50% higher than estimated? If LTV is 30%
lower? If we hire 2 more engineers? Give the numbers.

The sensitivity analysis is the part most founders skip — and the part that impresses investors the most. It shows you have stress-tested your own thesis.

Prompt #4 — The Investor-Readability Polish

AI prose tends to be 30% longer than necessary. Investors skim. This prompt compresses without losing substance.

Read the plan above as a partner at Andreessen Horowitz with
8 minutes to spare. Identify the 5 paragraphs that are most
likely to make you stop reading. For each:
  1. Quote the paragraph
  2. Explain why it loses attention
  3. Rewrite it in 2 sentences max, keeping any concrete
     numbers, removing all adjectives.

Then run the same exercise on the Executive Summary: it
must be ≤ 180 words and must contain: problem, solution,
market size, traction, ask. If any of those 5 elements is
missing, flag it.

Finally, replace any instance of the words "leverage",
"synergy", "cutting-edge", "revolutionary", "best-in-class"
with a specific concrete alternative. Print before / after.

This is the secret weapon. It turns "we leverage cutting-edge AI to revolutionize HVAC operations" into "we use GPT-4 to auto-dispatch the 47% of HVAC calls that arrive outside business hours." One is fluff; the other is a fundable sentence.

The 3 mistakes that kill AI-generated business plans

Even with the best prompt for business plan generation, three failure modes show up in 9 out of 10 first drafts. Recognize them and fix them before you send.

Mistake 1 — Treating the output as final. LivePlan's team warned explicitly: "AI is fantastic for brainstorming, but that doesn't mean you can just cut and paste the results into your business plan." Every number must be sourced. Every assumption must be defensible. The prompt gives you a scaffold; the founder provides the truth.

Mistake 2 — Vague customer definition. If your prompt says "small business owners," the output will be generic. The Prompt #2 template forces you to specify "US HVAC companies with 5–50 techs" — that one change in specificity cascades through every section and makes the plan feel real.

Mistake 3 — Skipping the "Why Now?" closer. Investors always ask why this moment. If the plan doesn't end with a clear answer — a regulatory shift, a new API launch, a post-COVID behavior change, a macro tailwind — it reads as a generic exercise. Reserve 10 minutes at the end of Prompt #2 specifically for this paragraph.

Putting it all together — the 90-minute workflow

Here is the actual sequence. Block 90 minutes on a Sunday morning and run it end-to-end.

  • Minutes 0–20: Run Prompt #1 (discovery). Answer every question. Don't skip.
  • Minutes 20–45: Run Prompt #2 (master) with your answers pasted in. Get the 8–10 page draft.
  • Minutes 45–65: Run Prompt #3 (financials). Build the table and sensitivity.
  • Minutes 65–85: Run Prompt #4 (polish). Cut filler. Sharpen the executive summary.
  • Minutes 85–90: Read the whole plan once aloud. Fix anything that sounds like AI.

Ninety minutes. Eight to ten pages. Investor-ready structure. The bottleneck was never the writing — it was the framework, and that is exactly what the best prompt for business plan generation gives you.

Next steps

Want to extend this workflow into the rest of your GTM stack? The marketing side has its own prompt playbook — see our guide on the best prompt for marketing for the 5-prompt funnel sequence. If you're still calibrating your general prompt engineering instincts, the 9 tips for writing better Claude prompts piece covers the structural patterns that make any of these prompts 3x more reliable. And for the underlying craft — why role assignment + constraint stacking + output-format pinning beats "write me a…" every time — revisit the prompt engineering guide.

Best Prompt for Product Description: Convert Browsers to Buyers

If you sell anything online, the best prompt for product description is the one that turns a boring 3-bullet spec sheet into a 200-word page that answers the only question a buyer actually has: "What's in it for me?" The good news: the heavy lifting has been done for you. Copywriting frameworks like AIDA, PAS, and FAB have been converting browsers to buyers since 1898 — and they slot cleanly into a ChatGPT prompt. This guide gives you 5 copy-paste templates built on those frameworks, plus the 4 mistakes that make AI product copy sound like AI product copy (and how to fix them in one follow-up prompt).

Why most product descriptions fail

Walk through any Shopify store with 100+ SKUs and you'll see the same five phrases recycled: "premium quality", "high-performance", "designed for everyday use", "perfect for any occasion", "a must-have." They're not wrong. They're just empty. WordStream's roundup of common copywriting mistakes nails it: copywriters "craft content in a way that makes sense to them, not the audience they need to engage." A spec sheet tells you what the product is. A buyer wants to know what it does for them in their life — that's the entire job.

The 5 frameworks worth memorising are AIDA (Attention → Interest → Desire → Action), PAS (Problem → Agitate → Solution), FAB (Feature → Advantage → Benefit), PASTOR (Problem → Amplify → Story → Testimony → Offer → Response), and 4Ps (Picture → Promise → Prove → Push). You don't need all of them. Pick the one that matches the page length. A landing page wants PAS or AIDA. A product detail page (PDP) wants FAB. A category tile wants 4Ps in two sentences.

The anatomy of a conversion-focused description

Every good product description, no matter the framework, has the same four beats:

  1. The hook. A sentence that names the buyer's pain or desired outcome. Not a tagline — a problem statement.
  2. The bridge. One or two sentences of features translated into advantages ("5000 mAh battery" → "lasts a full weekend off-grid").
  3. The benefit cascade. 2-4 short paragraphs or bullets answering "so what?" for each feature. This is where most AI copy skimps.
  4. The call to action. A specific next step — not "buy now" but "add to cart" or "reserve your pair" or "start your 30-day trial".

Your prompt must explicitly request all four beats. If you skip the hook, the output reads like a brochure. If you skip the benefit cascade, the output reads like a spec sheet. The templates below bake all four beats into the prompt so you don't have to remember them.

Template 1: The FAB long description (200+ words)

Use this for your hero PDP. FAB is the workhorse of ecommerce — Feature (what it is), Advantage (what it does), Benefit (what that means for the buyer). The prompt below turns a 4-bullet spec list into a 250-word narrative. Pair it with our marketing prompt library for cross-sell and email follow-ups.

Role: You are an ecommerce copywriter who writes in the style of
Allbirds and Glossier — concrete, sensory, no hype words.

Product: {product name}
Category: {category}
Audience: {who is the buyer, e.g. "remote workers in their 30s"}
Key specs: {bullet list of 3-5 raw features}
Price point: {budget / mid / premium}

Write a 250-word product description using the FAB framework
(Feature → Advantage → Benefit). Open with a 1-sentence hook that
names the buyer's pain. Then for each spec, write 1-2 sentences
that move from feature to advantage to benefit. Close with a
specific call to action (e.g. "Add to cart" or "Pick your size").

Rules:
- No hype words: avoid "premium", "high-quality", "perfect",
  "ultimate", "must-have", "luxurious".
- Replace each feature with at least one sensory detail
  (texture, weight, sound, time saved, money saved).
- Use second person ("you"), not third ("the customer").
- Do not start with the product name.
- Output: one paragraph per beat, no bullet points.

Template 2: The PAS short description (60-80 words)

Use this for category tiles, ad copy, and the top of an email. PAS (Problem → Agitate → Solution) is the shortest framework that still converts because it forces the writer to articulate the pain before pitching the fix. The "Agitate" step is the one most prompts skip — and it's the one that does the selling.

Write a 70-word product blurb in the PAS framework.

Product: {product name}
What it replaces or fixes: {the old way}

Structure:
- Problem (1 sentence): name the specific frustration the buyer
  has before they find this product.
- Agitate (1-2 sentences): describe the cost of that frustration
  in time, money, or emotion. Make it concrete.
- Solution (2-3 sentences): introduce the product as the cleanest
  fix. Lead with the benefit, not the feature.
- Close (1 sentence): a specific next step.

Tone: conversational, second person, no exclamation marks.

Template 3: The bullet-stack quick description (5-7 bullets)

Use this for the spec section under the main PDP copy, or for Amazon listings where shoppers scan rather than read. Each bullet follows a "verb + benefit + detail" pattern so it scans as a benefit, not as a feature.

Write 6 product bullets. Each bullet must be 12-18 words and
follow the pattern: [verb-led action] + [buyer-facing benefit] +
[concrete detail or number].

Product: {product name}
Specs to cover: {list of 4-6 raw features}
Audience: {one-line persona}

Rules:
- Start each bullet with a verb, not "Featuring" or "Includes".
- Every bullet must mention the buyer — use "you" or "your".
- No duplicate benefits across bullets.
- Avoid: lightweight, durable, sleek, ergonomic, premium.
  Replace with: {3.4 oz, drop-tested from 4 ft, brushed aluminium,
  fits a 6'2 frame, 18-month warranty}.

Template 4: The SEO meta description (155 characters)

Use this for the Shopify SEO app, Yoast, or whatever tool controls your search snippet. Google rewrites 60% of meta descriptions, but the 40% it keeps almost always includes the primary keyword and a concrete number.

Write a meta description (140-155 characters) for:

Product: {product name}
Primary keyword: {the exact phrase the buyer searches}
Secondary benefit: {the one thing that beats your competitor}

Formula: "{Primary keyword} {with a twist}. {Benefit} {in your
specifics}. {1 number or guarantee}."

Examples of the structure to mimic:
- "Yoga mats for bad knees. Extra-thick 8mm cushion that won't
  bunch. 30-day home trial."
- "Cold brew coffee maker. Glass carafe, no plastic parts.
  Makes 1L in 4 hours."

Do not use a question. Do not start with "Discover" or "Explore".
Output exactly one line, no quotes.

Common mistakes that make AI copy sound like AI copy

You can paste any of the templates above into ChatGPT and get a usable first draft in 30 seconds. You can also get generic mush if you skip these four guardrails. Alex Cattoni's rundown of the 5 biggest content mistakes hits the same notes: copy that "talks about features, not benefits" and copy that "doesn't think mobile-first". Add to that:

  1. Skipping the "agitate" step in PAS. The default ChatGPT output goes Problem → Solution in 2 sentences. Force it to spend 2-3 sentences on the cost of the problem before the solution lands.
  2. Using vague quantifiers. "Saves time" is a feature. "Cuts your morning routine from 12 minutes to 4" is a benefit. Always swap vague for specific in the follow-up prompt.
  3. No sensory detail. AI defaults to abstract language. Force one sensory word per paragraph — texture, weight, sound, smell, temperature.
  4. Burying the CTA. A 250-word description with a single "Buy now" at the end underperforms the same description with a 2-sentence CTA block: who it's for, what they get, what happens next.

Putting it together

The fastest workflow: pick the framework that matches the page (long PDP → FAB, short tile → PAS, specs → bullet-stack, search snippet → SEO meta), paste in the matching template, fill in the 4-5 variables, and ship the first draft. Then run one follow-up pass with the "common mistakes" checklist above — that single re-prompt usually lifts conversion copy from generic to specific in under 5 minutes. For a deeper dive on writing prompt-to-prompt chains that work in marketing automation, see our email writing prompt guide — the same PAS-then-CTA pattern works for subject lines and body copy alike.

Best Prompt for Cover Letter: Get Past the ATS Robot

If you have ever pasted a job description into ChatGPT, hit "generate", and submitted whatever came out without editing, you are not alone — and you are almost certainly not getting callbacks. According to a 2026 TopResume survey, 67% of hiring managers can identify AI-generated cover letters, and 54% view them negatively. The recruiter reading your letter is doing the same pattern-matching that a ChatGPT cover letter copilot does: every applicant who opened with "I am excited to apply for the [Job Title] position at [Company]" gets mentally filed under "low effort" and moved past. Meanwhile, 75% of resumes are rejected by ATS software before a human ever sees them (Jobscan, 2023), so even a generic letter is competing with 200+ applications filtered down to a five-callback pile.

The best prompt for cover letter writing is not the one that produces the longest output. It is the one that turns a one-line job description and a thin resume into a 3-paragraph letter that survives ATS screening, passes the 7-second recruiter scan, and gets you into the phone-screen pile. This guide gives you a four-part framework, four copy-paste templates (entry-level, mid-career, career-changer, executive), and the three mistakes that turn a 10-second prompt into a rejection email.

Why Most ChatGPT Cover Letters Get Ignored

The problem is not that the model writes badly. The problem is the prompt. According to Cover Letter Copilot's 2026 analysis of 10,000 job applications, cover letters created with basic ChatGPT prompts had a 23% lower callback rate than those written with strategic prompting. The same survey found that 81.6% of recruiters have encountered AI-written cover letters in the wild, and only 3.3% were unsure whether a letter was AI-generated — they pattern-match the generic phrases instantly.

Three structural failures cause the drop. First, the prompt gives the model no audience anchor, so it defaults to the safest possible tone: enthusiastic, vague, and indistinguishable from 200 other applicants. Second, the prompt asks for everything at once — opening, body, close, metrics, "why this company" — and the model has to pick what to skip. Third, the prompt has no output constraint, so you get a 600-word essay when the recruiter wanted a 220-word letter. The fix is structural, not stylistic.

The 4-Part Framework: A.R.C.S.

A.R.C.S. stands for Audience, Role, Context, Shape. It is the minimum viable brief for any cover letter, and it works with ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini, and Copilot. Each part serves a specific failure mode:

  • Audience — tell the model who reads this (recruiter vs hiring manager) and what they care about. Prevents the "I am excited" generic enthusiasm.
  • Role — state the job title, seniority, and the single biggest problem the hire is meant to solve. Prevents the "this could be any job" feeling.
  • Context — paste the job description AND your resume, plus 2-3 quantified achievements. Prevents the model from inventing metrics.
  • Shape — specify length, paragraph count, tone, and the one thing you want the reader to remember. Prevents the 600-word wall of text.

Skip any one of these and the model falls back to defaults. With all four, you get a first draft that is 80% ready to send — and that is the entire point. The remaining 20% is yours: one company-specific sentence, one metric you actually have, and one proofreading pass.

4 Copy-Paste Templates

These four templates cover roughly 90% of job applications. Replace every bracketed placeholder with your real details — the prompt will not work as a fill-in-the-blanks without your specifics, because the model has nothing to anchor on.

Template 1: Entry-Level / Recent Graduate

Act as a recruiter at [company name] reading this letter for a [job title] role.

Audience: a hiring manager who will skim the letter for under 10 seconds before
deciding whether to read the resume. They care about: willingness to learn,
relevant coursework or projects, and a clear reason this company (not any
company).

Role: [job title]. The single biggest problem this hire solves is
[problem from job description, e.g. "helping our customer success team
respond to tier-1 tickets faster"].

Context: here is the job description — [paste]. Here is my resume — [paste].
My 2 strongest achievements: [Achievement 1 with a number, e.g. "led a
capstone project that reduced data-entry time by 30% for a local nonprofit"]
and [Achievement 2 with a number].

Shape: 3 short paragraphs, 220 words maximum. Paragraph 1: state the role
and one specific reason I want to work at [company] (not the industry — the
actual company). Paragraph 2: connect 1-2 of my achievements to the role's
top requirement, with the number. Paragraph 3: short close — one line on
availability, one thank-you. Tone: confident, not boastful. No clichés
("passionate", "thrilled", "excited to apply"). Do not start with "I".

Note the "Do not start with 'I'" line. This is the single most effective constraint — it forces the model to lead with the company or the role, which immediately signals that you wrote the letter for this specific application, not from a generic template. The audience framing ("act as a recruiter") also matters: it switches the model out of "helpful assistant" mode and into "someone screening 200 letters" mode, which changes the output tone noticeably.

Template 2: Mid-Career Professional

Act as the hiring manager for [job title] at [company name]. You are reading
this letter to decide whether to forward the resume to the panel.

Audience: a hiring manager, not a recruiter. They have already passed the
ATS screen and are looking for evidence of impact at scale, not enthusiasm.

Role: [job title], [seniority level]. The business problem this hire solves:
[paste the relevant 1-2 sentences from the job description]. The team I will
join is working on [paste from the job description or company blog].

Context: my resume — [paste]. Three achievements with metrics that map to
this role:
1. [Achievement with number, e.g. "led migration of 12,000 SKUs to a new
   PIM, cutting content-publish time from 4 days to 6 hours"]
2. [Achievement with number]
3. [Achievement with number]

Shape: 3 paragraphs, 250 words max. Paragraph 1: open with the specific
business problem from the job description, not with my background. Paragraph
2: pick the 2 most relevant achievements and tie each to a different
requirement in the JD. Paragraph 3: close with a specific reason [company
name] is the right next move (cite a recent product launch, funding round,
or initiative I can reference from the company's newsroom). Tone: direct,
factual, confident. Use the words the job description uses.

The "use the words the job description uses" instruction is the ATS-optimization lever. ATS systems do keyword matching, but more importantly, recruiters do too — when they read a letter that mirrors the JD's language ("cross-functional collaboration", "stakeholder management", "platform reliability"), the cognitive load drops and the letter feels aligned. This is the same reason our best prompt for resume uses the JD as a literal input — keyword alignment is half the battle.

Template 3: Career Changer

Act as a hiring manager at [company name] who has just read my resume from
[previous industry] applying for [new role] in [new industry].

Audience: skeptical but curious. They have seen 50 career changers this
month and are tired of letters that ignore the obvious question: "why this
field?" They want a 10-second answer.

Role: [job title]. The transferable skill they care about most:
[inferred from JD, e.g. "client-facing communication" or "project
ownership"]. The skill I will need to ramp up on: [inferred, e.g. "SQL"].

Context: my background — [paste resume]. The 2-3 transferable achievements
from my previous career that map to this role:
1. [Achievement with a metric, e.g. "managed 14 client accounts worth
   $2.4M ARR, with 96% renewal rate"]
2. [Achievement with a metric]
3. [Achievement with a metric]

Shape: 3 paragraphs, 240 words. Paragraph 1: state the role and answer
"why this field" in one sentence — make it specific to [company name], not
"your industry is exciting". Paragraph 2: reframe my 2 strongest
achievements as direct preparation for the top 2 requirements in the JD.
Paragraph 3: address the ramp-up skill honestly — one sentence on how I
plan to close the gap (course, project, mentor). Close with availability
and a thank-you. No clichés.

Career-changer letters fail when the candidate pretends the switch is not happening. The recruiter already knows — the resume says it. The "answer 'why this field' in one sentence" instruction forces the model to do the reframing work that most candidates skip, and the ramp-up paragraph (Template 3's paragraph 3) signals self-awareness, which hiring managers consistently rate as the top signal in career-changer hires. If you have ever struggled to explain a pivot in your own words, this template does the structural work for you.

Template 4: Executive / Senior Leadership

Act as a board member or CEO reading a cover letter for a [C-suite / VP]
role at [company name]. You have 30 seconds.

Audience: time-poor, evidence-driven. They have already seen 200 letters.
They are looking for scale, P&L impact, and a clear narrative for why
this candidate at this company at this moment.

Role: [job title]. The strategic problem this hire solves:
[paste 1-2 sentences from JD or board materials]. The mandate for the
first 18 months: [your inference, e.g. "scale revenue from $40M to
$100M while opening the European market"].

Context: my executive bio — [paste]. Three board-level achievements with
P&L or scale metrics:
1. [Achievement, e.g. "led turnaround of a $180M product line, returning
   it to 18% YoY growth within 9 months"]
2. [Achievement, e.g. "built and scaled a 60-person engineering
   organization across 3 countries"]
3. [Achievement]

Shape: 3 short paragraphs, 280 words max. Paragraph 1: open with the
strategic problem, not my background. Paragraph 2: tie 2 achievements to
the company's specific situation — name the metric, name the constraint,
name the outcome. Paragraph 3: short close on fit and timing. Tone: peer
to peer, not candidate to gatekeeper. No exclamation marks. No "I would
love the opportunity".

Executive letters are the easiest to get wrong with a generic prompt because the model's default tone is "candidate to gatekeeper" — deferential, hedge-heavy, and full of enthusiasm. The "tone: peer to peer" instruction in Template 4 overrides that default. Combined with the "30 seconds" audience framing and the P&L metric requirement, it produces a letter that reads as if it was written by someone who has been in the room, not someone who wants to be in the room. The same peer-tone pattern is why our best prompt for email writing guide treats audience framing as the single highest-leverage move — it changes the model's calibration on every other line.

3 Mistakes to Avoid

1. The "Write me a cover letter" prompt. This is the equivalent of asking a stranger to introduce you at a networking event with no context — you get the safest possible version, which is the version 200 other people also got. The Cover Letter Copilot survey found that 67% of hiring managers identify AI-generated letters on tone alone, and the most reliable signal is generic enthusiasm ("I am excited to apply", "I believe I would be a great fit") with no specifics. The fix is the A.R.C.S. framework: every input you skip is a dimension the model has to invent, and what it invents is always generic.

2. Pasting the job description without your resume. The model can only connect your experience to the role's requirements if it knows what your experience is. Without your resume, it falls back to placeholder phrases like "my background in [field]" — which is a tell. The fix is to always paste both the JD and your resume, plus 2-3 quantified achievements, in the same prompt. If you do not have metrics, write down the roughest possible number ("managed roughly 10 client accounts", "worked on a project with about 30 stakeholders") — the model is much better at anchoring a real number than it is at inventing one.

3. Sending the first draft. The first draft is a draft. Plan to edit 40-60% of it — add one company-specific sentence (cite a recent news item, product launch, or initiative), swap in one real metric, and read it aloud once. The reading-aloud step catches the awkwardness that pure proofreading misses: sentences that are grammatically correct but rhythmically off, the kind of phrasing a model produces when it is over-fitting to formal cover-letter templates. The recruiter spends 7.4 seconds on the initial scan (Ladders, 2018); a letter that reads smoothly in 7.4 seconds is the only one that gets the next 60.

Put It All Together

The full A.R.C.S. pipeline runs in this order:

  1. JD + Resume Ingest — paste both into the prompt, plus 2-3 achievements with metrics. This is the "context" layer.
  2. Template Selection — pick Template 1, 2, 3, or 4 based on your seniority and situation. The template's "audience" and "shape" lines do most of the structural work.
  3. First Draft — generate, then read aloud. Replace any sentence that does not pass the "could only have been written for this company" test.
  4. Final Pass — one company-specific sentence, one real metric, one proofread for typos. Total time: 4-6 minutes per application.

For most job applications, the entire pipeline takes under 6 minutes per role — versus the 30-45 minutes most candidates spend on a "from-scratch" cover letter that still reads generic. The output passes ATS keyword matching, survives the 7-second recruiter scan, and reads as if it was written by a person who actually wanted this specific job at this specific company. That is what makes a prompt the best prompt for cover letter — not cleverness, but structure. If you want a deeper look at how Claude, ChatGPT, and Gemini each handle the A.R.C.S. framework, our ChatGPT vs Claude comparison walks through the same templates on each model.

Best Prompt for SEO: Rank on Page 1 in 2026

If you have ever pasted a target keyword into ChatGPT, hit "write me an SEO article," and shipped whatever came out, you are not alone — and you are not ranking. According to Ahrefs' 2025 study of 55.8M Google AI Overviews, the presence of an AI Overview correlates with a 58% lower click-through rate for the top-ranking organic result, and AI Overviews now surface on roughly 48–54% of all Google queries depending on the study. The "AI Overview + 10 blue links" SERP has replaced the "10 blue links" SERP, which means the bar for what counts as a ranking article has moved — it is no longer enough to publish a 2,000-word blog post with the keyword in the title. The model has to be told what to write, how to structure it for AI citation, and which mistakes to avoid that get the post filtered out of both Google's classical ranking and its new AI Overview citation pool.

The best prompt for SEO in 2026 is a structured, 5-part prompt that converts a single target keyword into a search-intent-matched, E-E-A-T-signal-rich, AI-citation-ready article brief — and then writes it. This guide gives you the framework, 4 copy-paste templates (the master prompt, the article rewriter, the meta-description generator, and the content-refresh prompt), and the 3 mistakes that turn a 10-second prompt into a page that Google Helpful Content Update never indexes. The structure is the same 5-part anatomy as our general prompt engineering guide, tuned specifically for the way Google's helpful-content classifier and AI Overview citation algorithm read content in 2026. For the writing-side prompt patterns (the actual word-by-word output), see Claude vs GPT-5 vs Gemini prompting; for the marketing-side prompts (funnels, ads, landing pages), see Best Prompt for Marketing.

What "SEO Prompt" Actually Means in 2026

An "SEO prompt" is not a magic phrase you paste into ChatGPT that guarantees a #1 ranking. No prompt does that, and any prompt that claims to is selling the same snake oil Google cracked down on with the August 2024 core update ("created just for SEO purposes" was the wording in Google's official guidance). An SEO prompt is a structured instruction that gets the LLM to produce content matching the four signals Google's ranking system uses to decide whether a page deserves a top slot — and now also matches the three extra signals Google's AI Overview citation layer uses to decide whether to quote your page inside the AI-generated summary at the top of the SERP.

Those four classical ranking signals are: search intent match (does the page answer the question the searcher actually typed?), topical depth (does it cover the sub-questions a knowledgeable reader would expect?), E-E-A-T demonstration (Experience, Expertise, Authoritativeness, Trustworthiness), and technical SEO hygiene (crawlable, fast, schema-marked, internal-linked). The three AI Overview citation signals add: structured quotability (are the answers in clean extractable blocks — definitions, lists, tables — rather than buried in prose?), freshness (does the page show a visible last-updated date and recent statistics?), and entity clarity (does the page unambiguously establish who, what, where, and how?). A prompt that hits all seven will rank; a prompt that hits only the first four will rank in classical results but get skipped by the AI Overview layer — and in 2026, that is half your potential clicks.

💡 The 58% CTR cliff is the single biggest SEO shift of the decade. Ahrefs' data shows that when an AI Overview appears above your page, your organic CTR drops by an average of 58% — and for some query categories, the drop is closer to 75%. The fix is not to avoid AI Overviews (you cannot), it is to be the source the AI Overview cites. A page that AI Overviews quote inside the summary box still gets branded visibility and a smaller but still-meaningful click stream; a page that AI Overviews skip gets nothing.

The 5-Part SEO Prompt Framework (S.E.A.R.C.H.)

Every SEO prompt that produces a page that ranks follows the same five-part structure. Skip any one of them and the model fills the gap with training-data default — which is a 2,000-word, vaguely-related, helpful-assistant-voiced article that neither ranks nor gets cited. The acronym is S.E.A.R.C.H.: Subject, Entity, Angle, Reader, Constraints, Hit-list. (The sixth "H" is the AI-Overview-citation brief, which we add below.)

  1. S — Subject & Search Intent: the exact keyword + the intent category (informational, commercial, transactional, navigational) + the format Google rewards for that intent (how-to, listicle, comparison, definition, review).
  2. E — Entity & E-E-A-T Signals: who is publishing this, what credentials they have, what first-hand experience they bring, and which authoritative sources they will cite. This is the section most prompts skip and the one Google's helpful-content classifier weights most heavily.
  3. A — Angle & Differentiation: the unique take, data point, or contrarian position that makes this article not a paraphrase of the top 10 existing results. Without this, the model writes a generic "10 tips" article that adds zero new information.
  4. R — Reader & Reading Level: who is reading, what they already know, what jargon is OK, and what the desired reading-level grade (Flesch 60–70 for most web content).
  5. C — Constraints & Output Contract: word count, heading depth (H2 / H3 / H4), number of internal/external links, tone, and the exact list of required sections (TL;DR, FAQ, key takeaways, comparison table).
  6. H — Hit-list for AI Overview Citation: the 3–5 extractable blocks (definitions, numbered steps, comparison tables, statistics with citations) the model must include so the AI Overview citation layer has clean quotable material.

1. The Master SEO Prompt: From 1 Keyword to a 2,000-Word Rankable Article

This is the master template. Paste it into ChatGPT, Claude, or Gemini, fill in the 7 bracketed slots, and ship a draft that is structurally aligned with both classical ranking and AI Overview citation. It is the same prompt structure we use for the marketing articles on this site, with the entity and hit-list sections added for SEO.

ROLE: You are a senior SEO content strategist with 10 years
of experience writing long-form articles that rank on
page 1 of Google AND get cited inside Google AI Overviews.
You write in clear, direct, US-English, Flesch reading
grade 60-70. You never use corporate jargon.

SUBJECT:
- Target keyword: [EXACT KEYWORD, e.g., "best prompt for SEO"]
- Search intent: [informational | commercial | transactional]
- Desired Google format: [how-to | listicle | comparison |
  definition | review]
- Word count: 2000 words

ENTITY (E-E-A-T):
- Publisher: PromptLab (https://prompt-lab.xyz)
- Author expertise: AI prompt engineering, SEO content,
  on-page optimization, AI Overview citation patterns
- First-hand experience: we run a 50-article SEO test site
  and have shipped prompts that rank in the top 10 for
  12+ competitive keywords
- Sources to cite: [Ahrefs, Google Search Central,
  Search Engine Land, Semrush — minimum 3 with year+stat]

ANGLE:
- Unique take: [the contrarian or data-backed angle that
  makes this NOT a paraphrase of the top 10 results.
  e.g., "Most SEO prompts fail because they target the
  keyword without targeting the search intent behind it"]

READER:
- Audience: [e.g., content marketers, indie SaaS founders,
  freelance SEO writers]
- Knowledge level: intermediate
- What they already know: what ChatGPT is, basic keyword
  research
- What they need to learn: how to structure a prompt that
  produces a ranking article in one shot

CONSTRAINTS:
- Required sections (in this order):
  1. Hook paragraph with the primary keyword in the
     first 100 words
  2. 6-8 H2 sections, each 2-3 paragraphs
  3. 4 <pre><code> blocks (one per copy-paste template)
  4. 1 comparison table (Markdown)
  5. FAQ section (4-6 Q&A)
  6. Key takeaways box at the end
- Internal links: 4-5 to other articles on prompt-lab.xyz
- External links: 3+ to authoritative SEO sources
- Tone: practical, peer-to-peer, no fluff

HIT-LIST for AI Overview citation:
- 1 definition block ("[keyword] is...")
- 1 numbered step list (5-8 steps)
- 1 comparison table
- 3 statistics with [Source, Year] citation
- 1 "pros vs cons" or "before vs after" block

The ROLE block is doing more work than it looks. Without it, the model defaults to its "helpful assistant" persona, which produces generic, hedge-everything content that does not get cited. The senior SEO strategist persona biases the model toward specific, opinionated, source-citing output — which is what both Google's helpful-content classifier and the AI Overview citation layer are looking for.

2. The Article Rewriter Prompt: Refresh Old Posts for the Helpful Content Update

Ahrefs' 2025 content-refresh case study shows that a single rewrite of an underperforming post can add 65% more organic traffic in 4–6 weeks. The recipe is the same S.E.A.R.C.H. framework, but applied to existing content instead of a new keyword. Paste the old article, the target keyword, and the new search-intent data — the model produces a refreshed draft that ranks without losing the URL's existing authority.

ROLE: You are an SEO content editor specializing in
content refresh for the Google Helpful Content Update
era. You rewrite existing posts to add E-E-A-T signals,
fresh statistics, and AI Overview quotability while
preserving the URL's existing backlinks and topical
authority.

INPUT:
- Existing article (paste below):
  [PASTE THE OLD ARTICLE TEXT]
- Target keyword: [EXACT KEYWORD]
- New search intent data: [Ahrefs/Semrush SERP overview —
  what format does Google now reward?]
- Date last updated: [WHEN THE ORIGINAL WAS PUBLISHED]

TASK:
1. Read the existing article and identify:
   - Outdated statistics (replace with 2025-2026 data
     from Ahrefs/Search Engine Land/Semrush)
   - Missing E-E-A-T signals (add author bio, first-hand
     experience, source citations)
   - Sections that don't match current search intent
   - Missing AI Overview quotable blocks

2. Rewrite preserving:
   - The original H1 and URL slug
   - The URL's existing backlinks (keep anchor text
     intact)
   - The original meta description unless it no longer
     matches intent

3. Add (in order):
   - A visible "Last updated: [DATE]" badge at the top
   - A 1-sentence "what's new in this update" callout
   - 2 new statistics with [Source, Year] citations
   - 1 new FAQ section at the end
   - 1 new internal link to a related PromptLab article

CONSTRAINTS:
- Preserve 70%+ of the original wording (this is a
  refresh, not a rewrite)
- Add 200-400 new words, not 1000+
- Keep the same reading grade (Flesch 60-70)
- Do NOT change the H1 unless it no longer matches
  search intent

The "preserve 70%+ of original wording" constraint is not arbitrary — it is what protects the URL's accumulated PageRank. A full rewrite (even a better one) loses the link equity the old URL earned; a surgical refresh keeps it and adds new ranking signals on top.

3. The Meta Description + Title Tag Prompt: Click-Worthy SERP Snippets

Title tags and meta descriptions are the single highest-ROI SEO element to prompt-engineer because they directly determine the click-through rate, and CTR is now a confirmed ranking signal (the 2024 Google API leak confirmed Navboost uses clicks as a quality signal). Most SEO prompts skip this step and ship generic titles — which is why most AI-generated articles get identical, generic, low-CTR SERP snippets.

ROLE: You are a SERP copywriting specialist who writes
title tags and meta descriptions that outperform the
current top 10 results in CTR.

INPUT:
- Target keyword: [EXACT KEYWORD]
- Current top 10 titles (from Ahrefs/Semrush SERP):
  [PASTE TOP 10 TITLES]
- Current top 10 meta descriptions:
  [PASTE TOP 10 META DESCRIPTIONS]
- Article's unique angle: [THE DIFFERENTIATION]
- Brand: PromptLab

OUTPUT (generate 10 options each):

1. TITLE TAGS (50-60 characters):
   - Format A: [Primary Keyword]: [Benefit] ([Year])
   - Format B: How to [Achieve Outcome] (Step-by-Step)
   - Format C: [Number] [Prompts/Templates/Tips] for
     [Audience] ([Year])
   - Format D: [Keyword] — [Contrarian Take or Data Point]
   - Format E: [Question]? [Short Answer] ([Year] Data)

   For each, give:
   - The title text
   - Why it should outperform the current top 10
     (what specific pattern is it breaking?)

2. META DESCRIPTIONS (140-155 characters):
   - Format A: Benefit + proof + CTA
     ("[Outcome] in [timeframe]. [Number] copy-paste
     [templates] tested on [tools]. [Free/Download].")
   - Format B: Question + answer + differentiation
     ("[Question]? [1-sentence answer]. [Why this is
     different from the top 10 results].")
   - Format C: Statistic + benefit + proof
     ("[Stat]% of [audience] [do X]. Here's the [number]-
     step fix that [outcome]. Tested on [tools].")

   For each, give:
   - The meta description text
   - The character count
   - The keyword placement (must be in the first 120 chars)

4. The Content Audit Prompt: Find What to Update Next

This is the fourth template and the one that closes the loop — instead of writing new content, it tells you which of your existing posts are losing traffic to the Helpful Content Update / AI Overview shift and what to do about them. Use it monthly to keep a content portfolio fresh.

ROLE: You are an SEO content auditor. You analyze a list
of existing URLs against current SERP data and produce
a prioritized refresh queue.

INPUT:
- Site: prompt-lab.xyz
- URL list (paste 10-30 URLs below):
  [PASTE URLS WITH TITLE + TARGET KEYWORD]
- Traffic data (paste Ahrefs/Search Console export):
  [PASTE CSV OR TABLE: URL, clicks last 30d, impressions
  last 30d, avg position, target keyword]
- Date range for trend: [e.g., last 90 days]

TASK:
For each URL, classify into ONE of these buckets:

1. WINNER (clicks up, position up): Do nothing. Maybe
   add 1 internal link from a newer post.

2. STEADY (clicks flat, position 4-20): Add 1 internal
   link, refresh 2 outdated stats, add 1 FAQ section.
   Effort: 30 min. Expected lift: 10-20%.

3. SLIPPING (clicks down 20-50%, position down 2-5):
   Full content refresh per the rewriter prompt above.
   Effort: 2 hours. Expected lift: 30-65%.

4. DEAD (clicks down 80%+, position 20+): Three options:
   a) Merge into a stronger URL (301 redirect)
   b) Full rewrite on a new angle
   c) Delete and 410 if no backlinks (rarely the right
      move — usually (a) is)

5. AI OVERVIEW VICTIM (rank stable but CTR dropped 40%+):
   Add 3 quotable blocks (definition, numbered steps,
   comparison table) and add visible "Last updated" date.
   This recovers ~30% of lost CTR.

OUTPUT:
- Prioritized refresh queue, sorted by expected traffic
  lift per hour of effort
- For each SLIPPING or DEAD URL, a 1-sentence diagnosis
  of what specifically changed (intent shift? AI Overview
  appeared? competitor published? stats outdated?)
- For each AI OVERVIEW VICTIM, the 3 specific blocks to
  add

The 3 Mistakes That Get AI SEO Content Filtered Out

These three mistakes account for roughly 80% of the AI-generated SEO articles that never rank. The first is the most common.

Mistake 1: No Entity, No E-E-A-T. The single biggest reason AI-generated SEO content gets filtered out of both classical ranking and AI Overview citation is that it has no demonstrable author entity, no first-hand experience, no source citations, and no obvious expertise signal. Google's helpful-content classifier is trained to spot "content created just for SEO purposes" — and the marker it uses is the absence of any of the E-E-A-T signals. The fix is the ENTITY block in the master prompt: name the author, list their credentials, declare the first-hand experience, and cite at least 3 authoritative sources. Without this block, the model writes the kind of generic "10 tips" article that gets deindexed in the next core update.

Mistake 2: Keyword stuffing in the prompt itself. New SEO users often "stuff" the target keyword 5–10 times in the prompt, thinking it will make the output rank better. It does the opposite — the model produces an output where the keyword density is unnatural, which the helpful-content classifier reads as a manipulation signal. The correct pattern is to mention the target keyword exactly once in the SUBJECT block, and then describe the search intent in natural language. The model's output will naturally include the keyword 3–7 times in 2,000 words, which is the density Google's helpful-content classifier rewards.

Mistake 3: No AI Overview quotable blocks. In 2026, a 2,000-word SEO article without a clean definition block, a numbered step list, a comparison table, and 2–3 cited statistics will rank in classical results but get skipped by the AI Overview citation layer — losing half your potential CTR. The HIT-LIST block in the master prompt exists to fix exactly this. Every AI Overview citation begins with the model extracting a clean, structured block of text from a source page — and if your page does not have one, the AI Overview cannot cite it.

Related: The 5-Part Prompt Engineering Guide — the underlying 5-part structure (Role, Context, Task, Constraints, Format) that the S.E.A.R.C.H. SEO framework is built on, with examples for ChatGPT, Claude, and Gemini and the same anti-pattern discipline that prevents fabricated statistics and corporate-jargon drift.
Related: Best Prompt for Marketing: 10 Templates That Convert — the 10 copy-paste marketing prompts (ad copy, landing pages, email sequences, sales scripts) built on the same S.E.A.R.C.H. framework, for when the SEO article needs to hand off to a conversion-focused funnel.
Related: Claude vs GPT-5 vs Gemini Prompting: Which Wins for SEO? — the model-by-model breakdown of which LLM produces the best SEO article drafts on the same prompt: Claude for E-E-A-T voice and source citation, GPT-5 for structured data and schema, Gemini for real-time freshness and Google-format awareness.

Best Prompt for YouTube Script: Hook + Payoff in 8 Minutes

Writing a best prompt for YouTube script isn't about asking an AI to "write me a video." That's the fastest way to get 800 words of beige copy that loses viewers in five seconds. The real best prompt for YouTube script work is structural: it forces the model to commit to a hook pattern, a payoff, and a CTA before it ever writes a sentence of body. Without that scaffolding, even ChatGPT, Claude, and Gemini default to generic intros that read like a Wikipedia summary.

YouTube's algorithm has been ruthlessly clear about what it rewards: average view duration, click-through rate, and audience retention curve. As of 2026, the average YouTube video holds viewers for only about 41.9% of its runtime, and Shorts lose 40–60% of their audience before the 15-second mark. That means the first 5–15 seconds of every video do more work than the next five minutes combined. A good script prompt doesn't just give you words — it gives you a structure the algorithm can read.

Why most "best prompt for YouTube script" lists fail

Most prompt libraries online are written for bloggers, not creators. They suggest things like "write a YouTube script about productivity" and call it a day. That kind of prompt produces generic intros: "Hey guys, welcome back to my channel. Today we're going to talk about…" — which the ClipHorizon team correctly calls "permission to swipe." The viewer hasn't been given a reason to stay; they've been given an exit ramp.

The other common failure mode is treating the script as one monolithic block. A real YouTube video has at least four distinct jobs — hook, setup, value, close — and each one needs a different prompt or at least a different instruction set. If you give the model one prompt and ask for "the whole script," you'll get a script that's optimized for none of the parts. The strongest prompts chunk the work: one prompt per section, chained together with explicit handoffs.

The Hook–Promise–Payoff framework

The CaptainHook video breakdown nails the framework that every high-retention script shares: pattern interrupt → promise → payoff. A pattern interrupt is the unusual opening — a close-up of something dramatic, a counter-intuitive claim, a question the viewer didn't expect. The promise tells them why staying will give them value. The payoff is the part of the video that makes them feel the promise was earned.

Vague promises create doubt. Overpromising with a weak payoff creates churn. The prompt needs to force the model to articulate all three explicitly, in that order, before any body content is written. If the model can't fill in "what's the payoff?" in one sentence, the script isn't ready to draft.

You are a viral video copywriter. The video topic is [TOPIC].
Target viewer: [AUDIENCE: e.g., "beginner Python devs, 25-40"].

Step 1 — Hook (≤10 seconds spoken):
Write three variations of an opening hook that uses either
(a) a counter-intuitive claim, (b) a specific number, or
(c) a knowledge gap the viewer wants closed. Each hook ≤ 18 words.

Step 2 — Promise (≤8 seconds spoken):
Write one sentence naming the three concrete things the viewer
will be able to DO or UNDERSTAND after watching. No vague
benefits. Each benefit must be testable.

Step 3 — Payoff preview (≤6 seconds spoken):
Write a single line that teases the strongest moment of the video
without spoiling it. This becomes the B-roll or cutaway moment
at 0:08–0:14.

Output as labeled blocks: HOOK 1/2/3, PROMISE, PAYOFF.

After you've approved those three blocks, you feed them back into the body prompt. This is the "prompt chain" pattern: a first prompt that does strategy, a second that does execution. It mirrors how an actual screenwriter works — outline first, draft second.

Long-form vs. Shorts: two different prompts

A 12-minute YouTube essay and a 45-second Short are scripted with completely different logics. For long-form, the hook has to survive for 30 seconds because you have room for context. For Shorts, the entire value core is 30 seconds long, so the hook is the brand.

For Shorts, use the 4-part structure documented by the ClipHorizon team: Hook (0–3s) → Setup (3–8s) → Value Core (8–40s) → Close (40–end). The Value Core should be exactly one mechanism, one contrast, or one reframe — not four. If you have four points, you have four Shorts.

Write a 45-second YouTube Short script on [TOPIC].
Tone: [e.g., direct, slightly opinionated, no "hey guys"].
Format STRICTLY:

HOOK (≤3s, ≤10 words): a single line that creates a knowledge
gap OR triggers emotion strong enough to stop the thumb.
Forbidden openers: "hey guys", "today we're going to",
"so I wanted to talk about".

SETUP (3–8s, ≤15 words): one sentence validating the hook and
framing what's coming. No backstory.

VALUE CORE (8–40s, ≤80 words): exactly ONE of these:
- Mechanism: why X causes Y
- Contrast: what most people do vs. what high performers do
- Reframe: the thing everyone thinks is the problem isn't
Keep sentences ≤12 words. Insert [PAUSE] markers every 8–10
words for the editor.

CLOSE (last 3–5s, ≤15 words): resolve what the hook opened.
If the hook promised "the reason your Shorts die at 10s,"
the close names that reason.

Output as four labeled blocks.

For long-form, swap in the Hook–Pain Point–Promise opener from Prompt Sigma: hook, single empathetic sentence acknowledging the viewer's current struggle, then a concrete promise of the three biggest takeaways. That opener buys you roughly 20 seconds of attention — enough to get into the meat.

The four prompt templates

Below are four copy-paste templates you can use as-is. Each one assumes you'll do a research pass first — gathering outlier scripts, real audience pain points, and your own voice samples. As OutlierKit's Aditi put it: "ChatGPT scripts only sound AI-generated when the prompt is generic — feed real niche data and the output gets sharper." Replace the bracketed slots with specifics from your niche, not generic words.

Template 1 — Tutorial Script (8–12 min):

Write a YouTube script for an 8–12 minute tutorial video.

Topic: [SPECIFIC TOPIC, e.g., "how to use Claude's tool-use API"]
Skill level of viewer: [e.g., "intermediate Python devs"]
One thing they'll be able to do after: [VERB-PHRASE]

Structure:
- Cold open (0:00–0:20): demo the final result first, no intro
- Context (0:20–0:45): one paragraph on why this matters now
- Step 1 (0:45–3:00): walk through, narration in second person
- Step 2 (3:00–5:30): walk through, narrate tradeoffs
- Step 3 (5:30–8:00): walk through, narrate common mistakes
- Recap (8:00–9:00): three bullets, no fluff
- CTA (9:00–9:30): specific next video, not "subscribe"

Constraints:
- Spoken word count: 1,100–1,400 (≈150 wpm target)
- Each paragraph ≤ 3 sentences on screen
- No "in this video" or "let's get started"

Template 2 — Story-driven video (10–15 min):

Write a story-driven YouTube script using the 5-part structure:
Hook, Setup, Conflict, Turning Point, Resolution.

True story / case study: [SUBJECT]
Emotional core: [e.g., "an underdog founder who almost quit"]
Target arc length: 12 minutes spoken
Viewer's takeaway (one line): [e.g., "constraints force clarity"]

Open with the most dramatic moment (mid-action), not the
backstory. Spend 40–50% of total runtime on conflict and
obstacles. End with the emotional impact, not just facts.

For each section, output: TIME, NARRATION, VISUAL.

Template 3 — Comparison / "X vs Y" video (6–9 min):

Write a comparison YouTube script.

Tool A: [NAME]  | Tool B: [NAME]
Use case: [SPECIFIC SCENARIO]
Viewer's decision: [WHAT THEY'RE CHOOSING BETWEEN]

Required sections:
- Cold open (0:00–0:25): state the conclusion, then explain why
- Side-by-side demo (0:25–3:00): same task on both, screen-recorded
- 3-point comparison (3:00–6:00): speed, quality, cost — show, don't tell
- Verdict (6:00–7:30): pick one, justify in ≤ 60 words
- CTA (7:30–8:00): "if you're deciding between X and Y for [SCENARIO],
  here's the next video to watch"

Constraints:
- Neutral until the verdict; no strawmanning
- Show timestamps and on-screen labels for the demo
- Total spoken: 950–1,200 words

Template 4 — Listicle (8–10 min):

Write a listicle YouTube script. List of N: [e.g., "7 Claude
prompting mistakes I made in my first month"].

Required structure:
- Cold open (0:00–0:25): name the most painful mistake first
- Why this list matters (0:25–0:50): the cost of not fixing these
- Items 1–N (each ~60s): mistake → why it hurts → fix → example
- Recap (last 60s): the one fix to make today
- CTA (last 15s): pin a comment with the full checklist link

Constraints:
- Each item must have a concrete example, not generic advice
- No "tip #5 will blow your mind" hype language
- Spoken: 1,150–1,400 words
- On-screen: numbered lower-third for every item

The common mistake: one prompt for the whole script

The biggest mistake creators make is using one mega-prompt for the entire video. "Write me a 10-minute script about X with a strong hook and a CTA" sounds efficient. It isn't. The model has to juggle hook, structure, voice, length, CTA, and tone in one pass, and it optimizes for whichever constraint you forgot to weight. Usually that's length — you get a 1,800-word script that's well-structured but reads like an essay.

The fix is the chain pattern: research → strategy → draft → de-AI-ify. The OutlierKit guide documents this as the workflow used by full-time YouTubers running faceless channels. A first prompt asks for the Hook–Promise–Payoff blocks only. A second prompt takes those blocks and drafts the body. A third prompt takes the body and strips the AI-isms: "however," "moreover," "in conclusion," "it's worth noting that." A fourth prompt rewrites the close in your own voice, using a 200-word sample you pasted in.

Each prompt does one job. Each one is auditable. If a section is weak, you re-run just that prompt with a sharper instruction — you don't throw away 1,200 words and start over.

How to feed the prompt real niche data

The OutlierKit guide is blunt: a prompt that says [NICHE] works when you write "budget cooking creators in Jakarta" and fails when you write "food." The model has no idea what's working in your niche this week. It pattern-matches against the general internet. For creators scripting with Claude instead of ChatGPT, the 9 Claude prompting tips cover the model-specific differences. To get scripts that read like a niche expert wrote them, the same logic that powers any prompt engineering workflow. feed the model:

  • 3–5 outlier transcripts from competitors' top videos (drop them into the prompt as "examples of tone and pacing")
  • Real audience comments — copy 30 comments from your last video so the model sees the actual objections and vocabulary
  • One paragraph of your own voice — past the spoken intro of a previous video so the model can mimic sentence length and rhythm
  • One specific contrarian take — the model is great at defending a position it's been handed

That input transforms a generic "best prompt for YouTube script" into a niche-tuned one. You'll still edit the draft — always — but the first revision will be much closer to publishable.

Editing the draft: the de-AI pass

Even with great prompts, AI drafts have tells. The most common ones in 2026: "Let's dive in," "It's worth noting," "In today's video," "Whether you're a beginner or an expert," and the dreaded "Hope this helps!" The fix is a final pass prompt that scans for these and rewrites them in a register you specify. The same logic shows up in the prompt engineering workflow.

Read the script below. Replace every instance of these phrases
with nothing (cut them entirely):
- "let's dive in"
- "it's worth noting"
- "in today's video"
- "whether you're a beginner or an expert"
- "hope this helps"
- "with that said"
- "moreover", "furthermore", "additionally"

Also flag any sentence > 22 words and shorten it to ≤ 14 words
while keeping the meaning. Output the cleaned script with
[CHANGED] markers next to every line you modified.

Run that, then read the script aloud. The 150-wpm target means roughly 1,000–1,300 spoken words for an 8-minute video. If you're over, cut the recap section before you cut the value core. If you're under, the hook probably needs to do more work — extend the cold open, not the body. Pair this with a storytelling arc and you get videos like the ones covered in the storytelling prompt guide.

Putting it all together

The best prompt for YouTube script work isn't a single magic prompt. It's a chain: research → strategy blocks → body draft → de-AI pass → voice rewrite. Each prompt in the chain does one job, and each one is auditable. That structure is what separates scripts that hold the 41.9% average view duration from scripts that don't.

Start with the Hook–Promise–Payoff prompt above. Approve those three blocks before you ever draft the body. Use the 4-part Shorts prompt for any video under 60 seconds. Pick one of the four long-form templates — tutorial, story, comparison, or listicle — and feed it real niche data. End every script with a de-AI pass. Read it aloud once before you record. That's the whole system. To see the same pattern applied to long-form data videos, read our data-analysis prompt breakdown.

Related: Best Prompt for Marketing — write the CTA that converts, not the one that begs.

Best Prompt for Podcast: Prep, Prompts, and Shownotes

If you have ever opened your DAW, stared at a blank recording screen, and realized you do not actually know what to ask your guest in the next 40 minutes, this guide is for you. The best prompt for podcast work is not a single magic sentence — it is a small library of focused prompts that handle the four jobs every podcaster needs: episode planning, guest research, shownotes, and social clip repurposing. Use them together and you replace hours of scattered prep with a 20-minute workflow that produces a tighter show, better takeaways, and a website page that actually ranks.

Most podcasters either skip prep entirely and improvise (which is fine for solo commentary shows, but deadly for interviews) or they over-prep with 12-page dossiers that nobody reads on-air. AI prompts sit in the middle. They give you structured output you can scan in 5 minutes, then ignore the parts that don't fit the conversation. As one creator put it, "use AI before the mic, not instead of the mic" — the human still drives the interview, but the prompt does the boring research first.

Why podcast prep is the highest-leverage 20 minutes of your week

A 30-minute interview is the result of maybe 2 hours of total effort: 20 minutes of prep, 30 minutes of recording, 30 minutes of editing, 20 minutes of shownotes. The prep is the smallest block, but it has the biggest leverage — a guest who feels seen answers with depth, and a host with three killer questions gets usable quotes for five social clips. Skipping prep is the single most common reason prompts underperform: the human half of the loop is not ready to use what the model produces.

The other reason to invest in prompts specifically: shownotes are now SEO fuel. Google cannot index audio, but it can index a 200-word episode summary plus a timestamps block. Tools like ChatGPT, Claude, and Perplexity are also increasingly being used as zero-click search engines for "best podcasts on X" queries, and well-structured shownotes are the surface they pull from. So a good prompt for podcast shownotes is doing two jobs at once: helping listeners skim, and helping AI systems surface your episode.

Template 1 — Episode planning from a one-line idea

Most episodes start as a sentence in a notes app: "talk about creator burnout." That sentence is not enough. Feed it into this prompt to get a 3-act outline, a hook, and 3 candidate titles before you ever open the mic.

You are a podcast producer. I host [SHOW NAME], a [GENRE] show for [AUDIENCE].

Episode idea: [ONE-LINE IDEA]

Produce a planning document with:
1. Three candidate episode titles (under 60 chars, curiosity-driven, no clickbait).
2. A 2-sentence hook for the cold open.
3. A 3-act outline (setup → tension → resolution) with 2 bullet points per act.
4. Three "aha" questions — questions whose answer would surprise the audience.
5. A one-line CTA for the outro.

Format as markdown. Keep total output under 350 words.

Run it the morning of recording, then rephrase the hook in your own voice. The model is fast at structure; you are the one who supplies taste. If you want a script for the same outline, swap to a storytelling-style prompt and ask for a 600-word narrative draft.

Template 2 — Guest research and interview questions

This is the prompt that pays for itself on episode one. Paste a guest's bio link, LinkedIn URL, or a few paragraphs of context, and you get a research brief plus 12 questions ranked by likelihood of producing quotable answers.

You are a podcast interview producer. Research the guest and prepare me for a 30-minute interview.

Guest context: [PASTE BIO, LINKEDIN, OR 2-3 PARAGRAPHS]

Deliver:
1. A 120-word guest snapshot (background, known for, current focus).
2. Three "bridge" topics — points where the guest's work intersects with my show's themes.
3. Twelve questions ranked by quotability (1 = most likely to produce a memorable line). Mix:
   - 4 background questions (origin, craft, daily life)
   - 4 substantive questions (their unique POV on the field)
   - 3 wildcard questions (slightly uncomfortable, but revealing)
   - 1 closer (forward-looking, future, advice)
4. For the top 3 questions, give me a 1-sentence "setup" line to land the question naturally.

Do not ask questions the guest has answered 100 times. Push for angles only they can speak to.

The "ranked by quotability" instruction is the key part. Without it, you get 12 generic questions. With it, you get a shortlist that respects the guest's time and your listener's attention. Read the top 5 out loud, mark the two that feel most alive, and skip the rest. The model is your research assistant, not your teleprompter.

Template 3 — Shownotes and chapter timestamps

Once you have a transcript (Descript, Whisper, or your DAW will produce one in minutes), this prompt turns it into a publish-ready shownotes block. A strong shownotes page has six sections, in this order, and stays under 250 words so it scans on mobile.

You are a podcast show notes editor. Below is a transcript of episode [NUMBER] of [SHOW NAME].

TRANSCRIPT:
[PASTE TRANSCRIPT OR FIRST 2000 WORDS]

Produce a shownotes block with exactly these sections, in this order:
1. Episode title (under 60 chars, specific, no "Episode 47: " prefix).
2. One-paragraph summary (under 60 words, lead with the listener's takeaway, not the topic).
3. Three key takeaways (bullet form, one sentence each, no fluff).
4. Chapter timestamps in MM:SS format — 5 to 8 chapters, with a 2-4 word label each.
5. Guest links: name, role, link (if applicable).
6. One-line CTA: subscribe / leave a review / visit [URL].

Rules: no marketing speak, no "in this episode we discuss" openers, no keyword stuffing. Sound like a thoughtful human, not an SEO bot.

The reason this prompt works for SEO and GEO (generative engine optimization) is the strict structure. Perplexity, ChatGPT, and Google all parse markdown bullet lists more reliably than walls of prose, and the timestamps block gives them a clean content map. Skip the keyword-stuffing temptation — the model is biased toward it by default, so the explicit "no marketing speak" rule is doing real work.

Template 4 — Repurposing one episode into 7 social clips

The hidden economics of podcasting: one 30-minute episode contains 4-6 quotable moments, each of which can become a Reel, Short, LinkedIn post, or newsletter pull-quote. Manually finding those moments is the most-skipped step. This prompt runs the transcript and outputs ready-to-edit clip briefs.

You are a short-form content producer. I need 7 social clips from this podcast transcript.

TRANSCRIPT:
[PASTE TRANSCRIPT]

For each clip, produce:
- A 15-30 second quote (verbatim from the transcript).
- A 1-sentence caption (under 140 chars, no hashtags in the caption).
- A hook line to display on-screen for the first 2 seconds.
- A suggested platform: Reels, Shorts, TikTok, LinkedIn, or X.

Rank by virality (most surprising, most emotional, or most useful). Skip generic advice. Prefer moments where the speaker contradicts common wisdom or shares a specific number, story, or framework.

Output as a numbered list. No commentary before or after.

Pair this with a YouTube script prompt if you also publish long-form on YouTube — the same transcript feeds both pipelines, and you usually get one full short-script and 7 short clips out of a single 30-minute recording.

The mistakes that wreck all four templates

Most podcasters who try these prompts hit the same three walls. None of them are about the model — they are about how the human uses the output.

Mistake 1 — Treating the prompt output as a script. If you read the model's questions word-for-word on-mic, you will sound like a robot. The output is a research brief. Read it, mark the two best questions, then close the laptop and have a conversation. Listeners can hear scripted questions from a mile away.

Mistake 2 — Letting the model invent facts about the guest. LLMs hallucinate bios, books, and podcast appearances with total confidence. Always cross-check at least one specific claim (a book title, a company name, a date) against the guest's actual site or LinkedIn. One made-up fact in a 12-question brief will poison the whole interview.

Mistake 3 — Using AI voices to replace the host. Several major platforms, including Audible, currently reject AI-narrated content. Even where it is allowed, listeners detect synthetic voices and disengage. The prompt's job is to make the human faster and better prepared, not to remove the human from the show. Keep your voice in the show; let the model handle the research, structure, and shownotes.

Putting it all together — your weekly workflow

Here is the loop that turns these four templates into a working system:

  1. Monday: Pick the week's episode topic. Run Template 1. Save the 3-act outline.
  2. Tuesday: If there is a guest, paste their bio into Template 2. Read the top 5 questions; pick 3 to take on-air.
  3. Thursday (recording day): Record. Use the outline as a checklist, not a script.
  4. Friday: Run the transcript through Template 3 to generate shownotes, and Template 4 to pull 7 social clips. Schedule everything for the following week.

Total active time on prompt work: about 20 minutes per episode, plus 30 minutes of editing the output. Compared to the 2-3 hours most podcasters spend on prep and shownotes, that is a 50% time savings, and the output is more consistent. Run the loop for 8 weeks and you will have a backlog of 32 transcripts, 32 shownotes pages, and 224 social clips — that is a quarter of content, generated in 2-3 hours per week instead of 8-10.

Start with Template 1 on your next episode. The best way to learn a prompt is to run it on real material, see where the output falls short, and rewrite the instruction that failed. After three or four iterations you will have a private library of podcast prompts tuned to your show's voice — and that library is the real competitive advantage, not the model itself.

Related: Best Prompt for Storytelling — for narrative-driven solo episodes.
Related: 9 Tips for Better Claude Prompts — generic prompt-craft skills that improve every template in this guide.
