Best Prompt for Blog Outline: 5-Minute Article Blueprint

If you have ever asked ChatGPT or Claude "write me an outline for a blog post" and received a generic 6-bullet list that could belong to any article on the internet, this guide is for you. The best prompt for blog outline work is not a single sentence — it is a 4-part prompt that turns a one-line topic into a 12-section outline with H2/H3 hierarchy, search intent baked in, examples slotted into each section, and a clear CTA placement plan. Use it once and you replace an hour of staring at a blank Google Doc with a five-minute outline review.

The reason most AI outlines feel flat: the prompt gives the model nothing to push against. "Write an outline on X" is the equivalent of telling a junior writer "write something good" — the output mirrors the input's vagueness. HubSpot's 2026 content research found that titles with specific numbers get 35% more clicks than generic ones, and the same precision principle applies to outlines: the more constraints you bake in, the more useful the output. As one lead put it, "the prompt is the rubric; the model is the student."

What a good blog outline actually has to do

A blog outline is not a list of headings. It is a contract: here is what I will cover, in what order, and why each section earns its place. The job of the outline is to make writing mechanical. Sharp outline = draft is fill-in-the-blanks. Fuzzy outline = every paragraph is a fresh decision, and the article never ships. The best outline prompt treats the outline as the most important artifact in the workflow, not a throwaway checklist.

Modern SEO research from InfluenceFlow's 2026 guide backs this up: 6-8 H2 sections with H3 subheadings, 150-160 character meta description, and one clear H1 produce the structure that Google and Perplexity parse cleanly. Skipping the H3 layer is the most common reason AI articles lose to hand-written ones — the model flattens detail into one heading depth and loses featured-snippet chances. For a deeper look at how structure ladders into ranking, our SEO prompt guide walks through the S.E.A.R.C.H. framework that pairs with this one.

The 4-part master prompt (copy this)

This is the prompt to paste into ChatGPT, Claude, or Gemini. It assumes you already have a target keyword and a rough sense of the audience. If you don't, fill the placeholders with placeholders — but the model will give you back a sharper outline if you commit to at least the keyword and the audience.

You are an expert blog editor who writes outlines that ship.
Topic: [YOUR 1-LINE TOPIC]
Primary keyword: [EXACT KEYWORD, e.g., "best prompt for blog outline"]
Target audience: [WHO IS READING, e.g., "indie content marketers publishing weekly"]
Search intent: [INFORMATIONAL / COMMERCIAL / TRANSACTIONAL]
Article length target: [WORD COUNT, e.g., 1500 words]

Produce a complete outline with these 12 sections, in this order:

1. Working title (under 60 chars, include the primary keyword, curiosity-driven).
2. Meta description (150-160 chars, lead with the reader's takeaway, include the keyword once).
3. URL slug (kebab-case, keyword-first, no stop words).
4. Cold-open angle (1 sentence: the hook that earns the next 30 seconds of attention).
5. H2 section 1 — [angle that earns the reader's trust, not a definition of the topic].
6. H2 section 2 — [the core promise of the article, with a 1-sentence sub-promise].
7. H2 section 3-5 — [three supporting sections, each with 2 H3 subheadings].
8. H2 section on counter-arguments / common mistakes (1 section minimum).
9. H2 FAQ section — 3-5 questions readers actually search, each with a 40-word answer hint.
10. Conclusion angle (no recap; a forward-looking next step).
11. CTA placement plan — specify which H2 gets the primary CTA and which gets a soft secondary CTA.
12. Internal link slots — flag 3 places where a link to a related article would help the reader.

Output format: markdown. Be specific, not generic. No "introduction" or "conclusion" as section names.

Run this once, then read the outline out loud. If any section makes you think "I could have written that without the prompt," that section is the one to delete or rewrite. The model is biased toward safe, predictable structure; your job is to cut the safe parts and keep only the ones that earn the reader's time. If you want a related marketing lens, our marketing prompt templates extend this same outline pattern into ad copy and email.

Template 2 — Skyscraper outline for competitive keywords

When you're going after a keyword where the top 3 results already publish 2,500-word guides, a generic outline will lose on day one. The skyscraper pattern from the SEO world (popularized by Brian Dean) calls for matching competitor depth and then adding 20-30% more unique value. This prompt is the AI-native version: it asks the model to research what's already ranking and explicitly draft the gap you're going to fill.

You are an SEO content strategist. I am writing an article targeting the keyword:
[KEYWORD, e.g., "best prompt for blog outline"]

Step 1 — Analyze the current top 5 results for this keyword and list:
- The H2 sections they all cover (table-stakes topics I MUST include).
- The H2 sections only 1-2 of them cover (differentiation opportunities).
- The H3 subheadings under each section (depth signal).

Step 2 — Draft an outline that:
- Matches every table-stakes H2 (so I don't lose on coverage).
- Adds 2-3 unique H2 sections that none of the top 5 cover (my edge).
- Specifies where I will add original data, an expert quote, or a first-person example.

Step 3 — For each section, flag the "uniqueness note" — one sentence explaining how this section will be MORE valuable than what the top 3 currently publish.

Output as a markdown outline with the uniqueness note in italics under each section.

The "uniqueness note" line is the key part. Without it, the model just produces a competent generic outline that ranks somewhere on page 2. With it, the model is forced to specify what the article is bringing to the topic that isn't already there. In practice, this is where 80% of the outline's value lives — the table-stakes sections are easy; the differentiation sections are where you win or lose.

Template 3 — Repurpose an existing article into 5 new outlines

The cheapest way to build an outline is to start from something you've already published well. This prompt takes a high-performing article, extracts the structural pattern, then applies it to 5 new topics in the same niche. It is the workhorse of any editorial team publishing more than 2 posts a week, and it's the same pattern the AirOps blog outline workflows use at scale.

You are a content repurposing strategist. Below is an article outline that performed
well in our content calendar:

PASTE A WINNING OUTLINE OR ARTICLE STRUCTURE HERE — ideally one with at least
3 H2 sections, FAQ, and a strong CTA placement.

Task:
1. Extract the structural pattern (number of sections, ratio of H2 to H3, FAQ
   length, CTA placement, opening hook style) into a 5-bullet pattern brief.
2. Apply that pattern to 5 NEW topics in the same niche. For each new topic,
   produce a complete outline using the extracted pattern.
3. Flag which of the 5 new topics has the highest chance of outperforming
   the original based on search volume trends, competitor weakness, or angle novelty.

Output: pattern brief + 5 outlines + the ranking recommendation.

The reason this scales is the pattern abstraction step. Ask the model to "write 5 new outlines" and you get 5 mediocre variations. Force it to extract the pattern first, then apply it, and the new outlines inherit the structural advantages of the winner without copying its content. This is the single biggest leverage point in a content team's weekly workflow, and it costs you 10 minutes and one prompt.

The 4 mistakes that kill AI-generated outlines

Most AI outlines fail not because the model is bad, but because the prompt skipped a constraint. These four account for ~90% of "the outline was useless" complaints; each is fixable with one extra sentence.

Mistake 1 — Generic section names. "Introduction," "Benefits," "Conclusion" are red flags that the prompt did not give the model a specific reader to write for. The fix: replace "Write an outline on X" with "Write an outline for [specific reader] trying to [specific job]." The output quality jumps immediately because the model has a person to address.

Mistake 2 — No H3 layer. A flat H2-only outline loses the chance to win sub-question featured snippets and reads as a "thin" article to Google's Helpful Content systems. The fix: in the master prompt above, the H2 sections 3-5 each require 2 H3 subheadings. That single rule flattens the most common structural complaint about AI content.

Mistake 3 — No CTA placement plan. Outlines that forget the CTA produce articles where the writer either skips the CTA entirely or bolts one on at the end like an afterthought. The fix: ask for a CTA placement plan in section 11 of the master prompt. The model will tell you which H2 gets the primary CTA and which gets the secondary; you can override, but you won't accidentally ship a CTA-less article.

Mistake 4 — No internal-link plan. Articles without internal links are orphan pages in your site's SEO graph. The fix: section 12 of the master prompt asks for 3 internal-link slots. When drafting, fill those slots with real links to related articles. If you need a starter list of articles worth linking to, our prompt engineering guide covers the foundational frameworks that most of your related articles will reference.

How to turn the outline into a draft without losing the structure

The outline is a plan; the draft is execution. Most writers lose 30% of the outline's structure when they start drafting because they paraphrase headings instead of preserving them. Keep the H2/H3 hierarchy intact and let prose fill around it. If a section's heading no longer makes sense once you start writing, the issue is the heading, not the prose — fix the heading, not the paragraph.

A useful follow-up: paste the outline back and ask the model to "expand each H2 into a 150-word paragraph using only the points in the outline, no new ideas." This keeps the draft faithful to the outline without hallucinating new sections. Then de-AI: read aloud, cut filler, add personal examples. A 1,500-word draft built this way reads cleaner than a 2,500-word draft from a vague prompt.

Run the master prompt first, approve structure, then expand section by section. Resist asking for the full article in one shot — outline-and-expand gives a checkpoint after each phase and a tighter final piece.

Best Prompt for Summarization: Compress Without Losing Meaning

If you've ever pasted a 30-page PDF into best prompt for summarization workflows and gotten back a vague paragraph that misses the footnote that actually changes the conclusion, you've hit the same wall that frustrates researchers, analysts, and product teams every day. The best prompt for summarization is not a magic sentence — it's a 4-part structure that forces the model to preserve meaning instead of just compressing words. According to a 2025 analysis by DataStudios, structured summarization patterns consistently outperform "summarize this" prompts by a wide margin on long research documents, policy briefs, and whitepapers.

This guide gives you a copy-paste template that works across Claude, GPT-4o, and Gemini, plus the four mistakes that cause summaries to lose the very details that mattered. If you want a broader look at how each model handles prompts differently, see our comparison of Claude vs GPT-5 vs Gemini prompting styles.

Why Most "Summarize This" Prompts Fail

The problem with a bare prompt like "summarize this document" is that it gives the model no signal about what to preserve. A 2025 study cited by Appen found that up to 47% of contextual meaning is lost when AI systems are asked to condense text without structural guidance. That's not a model problem — it's a prompt problem.

Dropbox Dash's October 2025 guide on 4 mistakes when asking AI to summarize puts it well: "The goal of a good summary isn't just to shorten — it's to preserve meaning. When nuance, structure, or tone are lost, decisions based on those summaries can easily go astray." The fix isn't a longer model. The fix is a more structured prompt.

The 4-Part Summarization Prompt Framework

Every reliable summarization prompt has four blocks: Role, Source, Compression target, and Output format. Each block removes one source of ambiguity. Skip any one and the summary drifts. This pattern is the foundation of the 9 Claude prompt tips we publish for English learners — but it generalizes to any model.

Block 1: Role — Tell the Model Who It Is

Without a role, the model averages across all possible readers. With a role, it commits to a perspective. A research analyst will weight methodology; a product manager will weight customer impact; an executive assistant will weight action items. Same document, three different summaries — and all three correct because the role framed the trade-offs.

You are a senior research analyst who specializes in extracting
decision-relevant findings from long-form business documents.
You prioritize conclusions, evidence quality, and any caveats
that would change a reader's interpretation.

Block 2: Source — Anchor the Material

Always tell the model where the text came from and how long it is. A 2,000-word article and a 50-page whitepaper need different summarization strategies. Length anchors let the model choose the right compression ratio.

SOURCE: A 48-page market research report on enterprise AI adoption
in Southeast Asia (2025 edition), commissioned by a regional
banking consortium.

LENGTH: ~38,000 words. Treat the executive summary, methodology
appendix, and any flagged data tables as critical — do not omit
findings that appear only in tables or footnotes.

Block 3: Compression Target — Specify Length in Two Ways

Saying "short" is useless. Specify both word count and content shape. The shape is more important than the count: a 200-word TL;DR looks nothing like a 200-word executive brief.

Produce TWO outputs:

1. A 150-word executive brief organized as:
   - One sentence on the report's main finding
   - Three bullet points on supporting evidence
   - One sentence on the most important caveat

2. A 5-bullet "key findings" list, each bullet ≤ 25 words,
   ordered from most to least decision-relevant.

Block 4: Output Format — Make It Parseable

If you intend to use the summary in a slide deck, Slack message, or downstream pipeline, ask for a format you can paste directly. Markdown headings, JSON objects, and CSV rows all work; vague "paragraphs" do not.

FORMAT: Return valid Markdown with H2 headings matching the
two sections above. Use **bold** for the main finding sentence.
Do not add any preamble, explanation, or closing remarks outside
the requested structure.

The Full Template — Copy and Customize

Combine the four blocks into one prompt. This is the version that works on Claude Sonnet 4, GPT-4o, and Gemini 1.5 Pro without modification:

You are a senior research analyst who specializes in extracting
decision-relevant findings from long-form business documents.

SOURCE: A {LENGTH}-word {DOC TYPE} on {TOPIC}. Treat the executive
summary, methodology appendix, and any flagged data tables as
critical — do not omit findings that appear only in tables or
footnotes.

TASK: Produce TWO outputs:
1. A 150-word executive brief with one main-finding sentence,
   three evidence bullets, and one caveat sentence.
2. A 5-bullet key-findings list (≤ 25 words each), ordered from
   most to least decision-relevant.

FORMAT: Valid Markdown. Use H2 headings "## Executive Brief"
and "## Key Findings". Bold the main-finding sentence. No
preamble, no closing remarks.

CONSTRAINT: If any section of the source is ambiguous or
contradicts another section, flag it under a final H2
"## Open Questions" with one sentence per ambiguity.

The CONSTRAINT block at the end is not optional. It's the line that prevents the model from silently picking one interpretation when the source is contradictory — a failure mode Dropbox calls "misinterpreting ambiguous phrasing."

Common Mistakes That Still Lose Meaning

Even with a structured prompt, four errors show up constantly. Recognizing them is half the fix.

Mistake 1: Treating Appendix and Footnotes as Optional

Models are trained to weight main body text higher than appendices. But in real documents, the caveat that changes everything is often in footnote 7. The explicit instruction "do not omit findings that appear only in tables or footnotes" overrides that default. Without it, your summary may report a 12% growth figure while quietly dropping the footnote that says "based on a sample of 14 companies."

Mistake 2: Asking for "Bullet Points" Without a Count or Order

"Give me bullet points" produces 3 to 8 bullets of inconsistent length and no priority order. Decision-makers scan the first bullet and skip the rest, so the most important finding must be first. Always specify the count, the max length per bullet, and an ordering rule (most decision-relevant first).

Mistake 3: Forgetting Tone

A summary of an internal compliance memo should not read like a marketing blog post. Tone shapes interpretation. If the source is cautious and hedged, the summary should be too. Add a tone clause when it matters:

TONE: Match the source. If the source uses hedged, cautious
language ("may," "appears to," "preliminary"), preserve that
caution in the summary. Do not upgrade tentative findings
into definitive claims.

Mistake 4: Trusting a Single Pass on Long Documents

For documents over 20,000 words, even strong models lose track of entities by the second half. The Chain of Density technique from Adams et al. (ACL 2023) handles this by iterating: ask for a 5-pass summary where each pass adds 1–3 entities without increasing length. The result is significantly denser and more faithful than a single-pass summary.

CHAIN OF DENSITY (for documents > 20k words):
After the two outputs above, repeat the following 2 steps 5 times:
  Step 1. Identify 1–3 informative entities ("; " delimited)
          from the source that are missing from your previous
          executive brief.
  Step 2. Rewrite the executive brief at the same length
          (~150 words) to include those entities while keeping
          all existing content.
Return all 5 versions in order, labeled D1 through D5.

This pattern, validated by a human-preference study on 100 CNN/DailyMail articles, produces summaries that humans rate almost as dense as human-written ones — far denser than a vanilla "summarize this" prompt.

Adapting the Template for Different Document Types

The 4-part framework is stable; only the role and shape clauses change.

  • Meeting transcripts — Role: "executive assistant." Shape: "5 action items with owner and deadline, plus a 3-sentence summary of disagreements."
  • Legal contracts — Role: "contracts analyst." Add: "Flag any clause that differs from standard market terms."
  • Academic papers — Role: "PhD-level researcher." Shape: "Background (50 words), Method (50 words), Findings (100 words), Limitations (50 words)."
  • Customer feedback — Role: "product manager." Shape: "Top 3 themes with quote evidence and severity rating."

For translation work where nuance preservation is even more critical, see our guide to the best prompt for translation — the same 4-part structure applies, with a tone clause that emphasizes cultural context.

Verifying That Nothing Important Was Lost

A summary you cannot verify is a summary you cannot trust. After every summarization pass, ask the model to do a faithfulness check:

VERIFICATION:
For each bullet in your key findings list, quote the exact
sentence or data point from the source that supports it.
If you cannot find a direct quote, mark the bullet as
"UNSUPPORTED" and remove it.

Output the verified list as "## Verified Findings".

This verification step catches hallucinated findings — a documented failure mode in production summarization systems. SupportLogic's March 2026 benchmark of faithfulness across major models found Gemini 1.5 Pro at 94.1% faithfulness, Claude 4 Sonnet at 89.2%, and GPT-4o Mini at 73.6% on enterprise summarization. Even the best model benefits from a structured verification pass.

Putting It All Together

The best prompt for summarization is not a clever sentence — it's a 4-block contract between you and the model: who it should be, what it's reading, how much to compress, and what shape the output takes. Add a constraint block for ambiguous source material and a verification pass for high-stakes use cases, and you have a workflow that scales from a single article to a 50-page whitepaper.

Start with the template above, swap the role and shape clauses for your document type, and run a verification pass on the first three summaries you produce. You'll quickly see which clauses matter for your domain — and which ones you can simplify. The structure is the value, not the exact wording.

Related: Claude vs GPT-5 vs Gemini Prompting — which model responds best to structured summarization prompts?
Related: Best Prompt for Translation — same 4-block framework, applied to preserving nuance across languages.

June 29, 2026 · Tutorial · 6 min read

Best Prompt for Content Rewrite: Refresh Without Losing Voice

Best Prompt for Content Rewrite: Refresh Without Losing Voice

JUNE 29, 2026 · TUTORIAL · 6 MIN READ

Most rewrites of old articles fail for the same reason. The writer asks the AI to "make this better," and the AI obliges by smoothing out every sharp edge, every opinion, and every sentence that sounded like a real person wrote it. You end up with a "refreshed" post that reads like a slightly newer version of a generic blog — best prompt for content rewrite is the cure, and it is more about constraint than creativity.

The trick is to give the model three things at once: the original text, a description of the voice it had, and an explicit list of what counts as "better." Without that triad, the model defaults to the average of the internet. With it, the model becomes a careful editor instead of a copywriter. This guide walks through the 3-part prompt that turns a stale 2023 article into a 2026-relevant version while keeping the original voice intact, plus four variants for common rewrite jobs.

Why AI Rewrites Sound Generic (and How to Fix It)

Generative AI is trained on the average of the internet, which means its default output sounds like everyone else's. As Contentstack's Kevin Thomas puts it, "If you ask an LLM to generate copy for 'digital transformation' or 'customer experience,' it will give you perfectly serviceable, grammatically accurate prose that could have been generated by any company in your sector." That is the "sameness problem" — and it is most visible on rewrites, because the original was distinctive and the new version is not.

Brand voice is more than style. It is the set of inside references, opinions, and rhythms that make a publication recognisable. AI can simulate tone; it cannot recall the specific decisions that shaped a 2023 article. Your prompt has to do that recall for it. That is why the prompt below front-loads a voice description and a list of protected phrases before any rewriting happens.

The 3-Part Rewrite Prompt (Copy and Adapt)

Paste the following into Claude or any capable model, filling in the three bracketed blocks. The first block is the original article (or the section you want to refresh). The second block is a short voice brief — three to five sentences is plenty. The third block is the "do not touch" list of phrases, examples, and claims that must survive the rewrite.

You are rewriting a section of an existing article while preserving its voice.

ORIGINAL TEXT:
"""
[paste the paragraph or full article here]
"""

VOICE BRIEF:
- Reads like a practitioner who has shipped this work, not a generalist.
- Uses short sentences for claims, longer sentences for nuance.
- Favors concrete numbers, named tools, and dated examples.
- Opinions appear in the first or last sentence of a paragraph, never buried.
- Avoids: marketing adjectives, throat-clearing transitions, generic conclusions.

PROTECTED ELEMENTS (must appear in the rewrite verbatim or near-verbatim):
- The 2023 launch date of [product/feature]
- The quote from [named source]
- The phrase "[distinctive tagline or metaphor]"
- Any statistic with its original source citation
- The H2 headings in the order they appear

TASK: Rewrite the original text so it reflects 2026 context (new tooling, updated
statistics where the original is now stale, current best practices) while keeping
the protected elements and the voice brief intact. Do not shorten by more than 10%.
Return only the rewritten text, no commentary.

The protected-elements block is the part most people skip. Skipping it is why rewrites lose voice. The list forces the model to keep your distinctive phrases, your dated examples, and your named sources — which are usually the load-bearing parts of the original article.

Variant 1: Refresh a Stale Statistic Without Rewriting the Whole Article

Sometimes the article is fine; only one or two statistics are dated. A full rewrite is overkill. Use this surgical variant when you want to swap in current numbers without touching the rest.

You are updating statistics in an existing article section.

SECTION TO UPDATE:
"""
[paste the section, or just the paragraph with the stale stat]
"""

REPLACEMENTS (use these exact figures, with citations):
- "[old stat]" → [new stat] (source: [publication, year])
- "[old stat 2]" → [new stat 2] (source: [publication, year])

VOICE BRIEF: [paste the same voice brief from above]

TASK: Replace only the marked statistics with the new figures and citations.
Do not change sentence structure, voice, or surrounding content.
Preserve every other phrase verbatim.

This is the prompt to use when a reader emailed you about a 2023 number, or when a quarterly review flagged a stat that has shifted. A targeted stat-swap is a five-minute job with this prompt and a full rewrite is not necessary.

Variant 2: Add a 2026 Section to a 2023 Article

When the original argument is still correct but the field has moved, the right move is often to add a new section rather than rewrite the old one. Readers who bookmarked the post expect the original to still be there; new readers want the update. The "append" variant respects both.

You are adding a new section to an existing article.

EXISTING ARTICLE (read for context and voice):
"""
[paste full article]
"""

NEW SECTION TOPIC: [e.g., "What changed in 2026 for AI-assisted coding"]

VOICE BRIEF: [paste the same voice brief]

CONSTRAINTS:
- New section must sit between the existing [H2 heading] and the next H2.
- New section should be 200-300 words, one H2, no subheadings.
- Reference at least one 2025 or 2026 source by name and date.
- Open the section with a single sentence that names what changed, not "In recent years..."
- Close with one sentence that connects the new section to the original article's thesis.

TASK: Write only the new section text, ready to paste between the marked H2s.

The "no 'In recent years...'" constraint is doing real work. It blocks the most common AI opener, which would immediately mark the new section as filler.

Variant 3: Localize a Post for a New Market

When you are adapting a post that worked in one market for a different region, the rewrite is not about voice — it is about swapping examples, currencies, regulatory references, and tool names. The voice stays; the references change.

You are localizing an article for a new market while preserving voice.

ORIGINAL ARTICLE (US-focused):
"""
[paste article]
"""

TARGET MARKET: [e.g., "Indonesia", "Germany", "Brazil"]

VOICE BRIEF: [paste the same voice brief]

LOCALIZATION RULES:
- Replace US-only tool names with the local equivalent (e.g., Stripe → local payment processor).
- Convert all currencies and units.
- Update regulatory references (GDPR, OJK, HIPAA, etc.) to the target jurisdiction.
- Replace US-centric examples with local examples where possible.
- Keep the original H2 structure and order.
- Keep all protected elements from the protected list.

TASK: Return the fully localized article.

This is the variant to use when a 2023 post on "best payment tools for SaaS" suddenly needs to ship in three new markets. The voice brief is the same; only the local-knowledge layer changes.

The 4-Step Workflow for a Full Refresh

Run the 3-part prompt inside a four-step loop. Each step has a clear output, which makes it easy to spot when the AI has drifted from the voice brief before you commit the change.

  1. Audit: Read the original article and write down 3-5 phrases you would be sad to lose. These go into the protected-elements block.
  2. Draft: Run the 3-part prompt. Save the output as "draft v1."
  3. Self-check: Run the draft back through the model with this follow-up: "Read the rewrite against the voice brief. List any sentence that violates the brief, and propose a fix. Do not rewrite the whole article."
  4. Human pass: Read the output aloud. Anywhere you stumble, the AI broke the voice. Fix those spots by hand before publishing.

Step 3 is the one that gets skipped most often, and it is the step that turns a 70% rewrite into a 95% rewrite. The model is good at critiquing its own work when you ask it to check against a fixed rubric.

Mistakes to Avoid

Three patterns come up in nearly every failed AI rewrite, and they are easy to prevent once you know to look for them.

Buried lede. "Every refreshed article must clearly answer the user's question in the first 100 words," writes Johnny Hughes of Avenue Z. Most rewrites move the answer down to paragraph three because the model thinks that is more thorough. It is not. Force the new first paragraph to lead with the answer in the first sentence.

Stripped opinions. A 2023 article that took a stance gets rewritten into a 2026 article that hedges everything. The model is biased toward neutrality because neutrality is the average of the training data. Mark the opinionated sentences in the protected-elements list and the model will keep them.

Throat-clearing transitions. Phrases like "It's worth noting that," "In today's fast-paced world," and "Let's dive in" almost never appear in a strong original article, but they appear in nearly every AI rewrite. Add "no throat-clearing transitions" to the voice brief and the output tightens immediately.

Related: Best Prompt for Blog Outline — the planning prompt that pairs with this rewrite prompt for end-to-end content production.
Related: Best Prompt for SEO — refresh keyword strategy and on-page structure alongside the rewrite.
Related: 9 Tips for Better Claude Prompts — broader prompt-crafting techniques that make every rewrite prompt work harder.

July 1, 2026 · Templates · 6 min read

Best Prompt for Meeting Notes: From Transcript to Action Items

Most best prompt for meeting notes attempts fail for a single, fixable reason: they ask the model to "summarize this meeting" and stop there. The result is a polished paragraph that hides who owns what and when it's due. By the next morning, nobody remembers which decisions were final and which were "we should probably...". The fix is a three-part prompt that forces the model to extract structure, not just compress words. In this guide you'll get the exact prompt, three copy-paste variants for common meeting types, and a checklist of mistakes that turn transcripts into noise.

Why most meeting-notes prompts fall apart

Meeting transcripts have a particular shape. They are long, conversational, full of false starts, hedges, and side discussions. When you ask a language model to "summarize" a transcript, it does what summarization models are trained to do: it compresses prose. But what you need is not compression — you need extraction. You need a table of action items with owners and deadlines, a list of decisions separated from open questions, and a parking lot for topics that were raised but deferred.

Three things go wrong with the bare "summarize" prompt. First, the model merges decided items with discussed items, so a tentative comment from one participant looks like a team commitment. Second, vague ownership like "the team will follow up" gets reproduced verbatim instead of being flagged as unassigned. Third, deadlines like "soon" or "before launch" get smoothed into prose rather than being marked as unresolved, which then propagates into your task manager and quietly rots.

Key insight: Ambiguous ownership is the leading reason meeting decisions never get implemented. The prompt's job is to surface that ambiguity, not hide it.

The 3-part prompt structure

The best prompt for meeting notes follows a predictable anatomy: role + transcript + structured output schema. The role grounds the model's voice (executive assistant, project manager, or chief of staff). The transcript is the raw input. The schema is the most important part — it tells the model exactly which sections to produce and in what order. Without a schema, the model decides for you, and you'll spend more time reformatting the output than the meeting cost you.

Here is the core prompt you'll reuse for 80% of meetings:

You are an executive assistant. Transform the meeting transcript below into structured
meeting notes using exactly these sections. Use the actual names mentioned in the
transcript. Do not invent owners or deadlines.

## Meeting Summary
(2-3 sentences: what the meeting was about and the key outcome)

## Decisions Made
- Bullet list of every decision that was finalized.
- If something was discussed but not decided, it goes in Open Questions instead.

## Action Items
(Table with columns: Action | Owner | Deadline | Priority)
- If no owner was mentioned, write "UNASSIGNED" and flag it.
- If no deadline was mentioned, write "NEEDS DATE" and flag it.
- Priority: High / Medium / Low based on context.

## Open Questions
(Anything raised but not resolved.)

## Parking Lot
(Topics mentioned but deferred to a future meeting.)

## Notable Quotes
(Statements that should be captured verbatim — commitments, objections, key insights.)

Transcript:
[PASTE TRANSCRIPT HERE]

The "flag" instructions matter more than they look. They are the difference between a prompt that politely hand-waves and one that returns a document you can paste into Linear without manual cleanup.

Variant 1: 1:1 manager meetings

One-on-ones produce a different kind of output. The action items are often about people, not tasks: feedback, growth topics, blockers that the manager can unblock. Use this template:

You are a chief of staff. Extract structured notes from this 1:1 transcript
between [your name] and [their name, their role].

## Status Updates
(What they shared about their work and projects.)

## Blockers
(What is slowing them down, plus any solutions discussed.)

## Feedback Given
(Any feedback [your name] gave — for [your name]'s records.)

## Feedback Received
(Anything they flagged for [your name] to action.)

## Career and Growth
(Development topics, goals, growth opportunities discussed.)

## Mood and Energy Check
(How did they seem? Brief note on tone, energy, anything unspoken.)

## Action Items
(Table: Action | Owner | Deadline | Priority)
- Same flagging rules as the core prompt.

Transcript:
[PASTE TRANSCRIPT HERE]

The Mood and Energy Check section is the part most people skip — and the part most useful three months later when you are calibrating performance or simply remembering what was on someone's mind.

Variant 2: sprint planning and project meetings

Project meetings usually revolve around scope, risk, and ownership of new work. The action item table needs an extra column for sprint or week, and you usually want a "scope changes" section so the diff from the previous meeting is explicit:

You are a project manager. Transform this project meeting transcript into
structured notes for [project name].

## Meeting Summary
(2-3 sentences: goals of this meeting and the outcome.)

## Key Decisions
(Bulleted, with who made each decision.)

## Scope Changes
(Any additions, cuts, or modifications to project scope since last meeting.)

## Risk Flags
(New risks identified or existing risks escalated. Owner + mitigation if discussed.)

## Action Items
(Table: Action | Owner | Deadline | Sprint/Week | Priority)
- If a decision implies work that wasn't explicitly assigned, list it as
  "UNASSIGNED — derive from [decision]".

## Next Meeting
(Date, time, and any pre-work required.)

Transcript:
[PASTE TRANSCRIPT HERE]

The "derive from decision" line is a small but important escape hatch. Many project meetings make commitments in the form of decisions, and the model needs explicit permission to infer follow-up tasks from those decisions.

Variant 3: client and sales calls

External calls have a different constraint: you usually cannot share the transcript with the same AI in a way that includes proprietary customer data, and the output needs to land in a CRM, not a doc. For these, use a tighter prompt that produces a JSON-ish structure and explicitly excludes anything that could be a quote used out of context:

You are a sales operations analyst. Extract structured notes from this client call.

Output exactly four sections, in this order. Use the speaker names from the transcript.

## Summary
(2-3 sentences, factual, no editorializing.)

## Client Requirements
(Bulleted list of what the client asked for, in their words.)

## Objections and Concerns
(Direct quotes preferred. Mark each with the speaker.)

## Next Steps
(Table: Action | Owner (us or them) | Deadline | Priority)

Do not paraphrase objections into softer language. Verbatim is more useful than diplomatic.

Transcript:
[PASTE TRANSCRIPT HERE]

For client calls the best prompt for meeting notes is the one that preserves voice. A meeting summary that turns a sharp client objection into a polite hedge is worse than no summary at all, because it lets the team underestimate risk.

The follow-up email in one extra prompt

Once you have structured notes, the next highest-leverage use is a follow-up email. Most teams skip this because it adds ten minutes of writing. With the right prompt, it takes five seconds:

Convert the structured meeting notes above into a follow-up email.

Format:
- Subject: [Meeting name] — [Date] — Key Decisions & Action Items
- Open with a 1-sentence summary.
- List decisions as bullets.
- List action items as a table with owners bolded.
- Close with: "Reply to this email if any owners or deadlines need correction."
- Total length: under 200 words.

The closing line is the most important part of the email. It gives people a specific, low-friction way to correct the record. Without it, a recipient who disagrees with their assigned deadline just stays silent — and three days later you discover the deadline was never real.

Related: Best Prompt for Summarization — for non-meeting text like articles and reports. Best Prompt for SOP — for turning the decisions above into a runnable procedure. 9 Tips for Better Claude Prompts — the broader prompt-engineering habits that make all of this work.

Common mistakes to avoid

Even with a good prompt, three mistakes break the output. First, forgetting to paste the transcript. The model will happily hallucinate a meeting that never happened, with realistic-looking attendees and action items. Always check that the model is working from the actual transcript, not from its prior about "typical" meetings. Second, accepting "the team" as an owner. "The team will follow up" is not an action item. Require real names. Third, omitting the "do not invent" rule. Without it, the model will fill in plausible-but-fake deadlines for items where the meeting actually punted on a date. The flagging instructions work, but only if the model knows it is forbidden to invent.

A fourth mistake worth flagging: not specifying the meeting type up front. A 1:1 prompt run on a board meeting transcript will produce a Mood and Energy Check for the CFO, which is the wrong artifact. Always tell the model which kind of meeting it is reading.

Putting it into a workflow

The prompt is the easy part. The hard part is making the habit stick. Three small operational changes make this a 60-second task instead of a 30-minute cleanup. First, save the prompt once in a notes app or a Claude Project called "Meeting Transcripts" so you do not rewrite it every time. Second, paste the transcript within ten minutes of the meeting ending — context is fresh, and the model catches nuance you would forget. Third, treat the model's output as a draft, not a final. Read the action items table specifically: every "UNASSIGNED" and "NEEDS DATE" is a follow-up you actually need to do in the meeting room, not later in your task manager.

Used this way, the best prompt for meeting notes stops being a clever trick and becomes the default way your team operates. Decisions stop dying in Slack threads, action items stop being assigned to "the team", and the people who were not in the room can read two minutes of structured notes and know exactly what is happening. That is the whole point.

Related: Best Prompt for Summarization — for non-meeting text like articles and reports. Best Prompt for SOP — for turning the decisions above into a runnable procedure. 9 Tips for Better Claude Prompts — the broader prompt-engineering habits that make all of this work.

Best Prompt for SOP: Standard Operating Procedure in 30 Minutes

If you have ever asked ChatGPT or Claude to "write me an SOP" and received five pages of generic boilerplate that nobody on your team can actually follow, you already know why finding the best prompt for SOP is harder than it looks. Standard operating procedures are not essays — they are contracts. They tell a specific person what to do, in what order, under what conditions, and what to do when something goes wrong. Most prompts never reach that level of structure, which is why most AI-generated SOPs end up rewritten by hand anyway.

This guide gives you the five-part prompt that turns a 90-minute process-owner interview into a draft SOP a new operator can follow on day one. You will get four copy-paste templates, a check on the time savings you should expect, and the three mistakes that turn a 30-minute prompt into a 3-hour cleanup.

Why Most "Write an SOP" Prompts Fail

Most failed SOP prompts share the same three problems. First, they ask for the document when the model has no source material. The model fills the gap with generic structure ("Purpose: ... Scope: ...") that looks correct but is operationally useless. Second, they ask for everything at once — roles, decision tree, exceptions, compliance language, revision history — and the model produces a wall of prose that nobody can navigate. Third, they never specify the audience, so the model defaults to writing for auditors instead of operators. The result is a document that passes a compliance review and sits unread on a shelf.

Creating a well-structured SOP is a 4-8 hour task for a moderately complex process when done manually. With a properly structured prompt, that drops to 30-90 minutes, because the subject matter expert is reviewing rather than drafting. The fix is structural: hand the model the source material it would otherwise have to invent, and constrain the output to the shape a real operator needs.

The 5-Part Framework: R.O.L.E.S.

R.O.L.E.S. stands for Role, Objective, List of steps, Exceptions, Source. It is the minimum viable brief for any SOP-generation task, and it works in Claude, ChatGPT, and Gemini. Each part serves a specific failure mode:

  • Role — who is the SOP for, and who owns it. Prevents the "this is for everyone" generic draft.
  • Objective — what the process produces, in one measurable sentence. Prevents the model from drifting into a process tour instead of a deliverable.
  • List of steps — the source material: voice transcript, bullet notes, or existing rough draft. Prevents hallucinated procedures.
  • Exceptions — the "what if" cases the operator actually worries about. Prevents the SOP that ignores every realistic failure mode.
  • Source — the formatting template the model must follow. Prevents the prose blob and the inconsistent structure across documents.

For the broader prompting principles behind this framework, see our prompt engineering guide. R.O.L.E.S. is what general prompting discipline looks like once it meets a regulated process document.

Template 1 — The Blank-Slate Draft

Use this when you have no existing SOP and the only source material is a 10-minute conversation with the process owner. The prompt forces the model to extract structure from messy input and produces a first draft in under 5 minutes.

Role: You are an operations writer who specialises in turning verbal
process knowledge into auditable SOPs for non-technical operators.

Objective: Produce a complete SOP for [process name] that a new
employee can follow on day one with no prior context.

List of steps (source material — paste a voice transcript, bullet
notes, or rough draft below this line):
---
[PASTE YOUR SOURCE MATERIAL HERE]
---

Exceptions: Ask me up to 5 clarifying questions about edge cases
BEFORE you draft — for example, what happens when [input] is missing,
or when [step] fails. Number your questions.

Source (required structure):
1. Title and document owner
2. Purpose (2-3 sentences, plain English)
3. Scope (what is and is not covered)
4. Responsibilities (role, not person)
5. Prerequisites (tools, access, training)
6. Procedure (numbered steps, one action per step)
7. Decision tree for the most likely branch point
8. Edge cases and escalation
9. Revision history table (placeholder)

Constraints:
- One action per numbered step.
- Use present tense, active voice, second person ("Open the file").
- No acronyms without expansion on first use.
- Flag any step where the source material is ambiguous.

The "ask clarifying questions first" instruction is the secret ingredient. It forces the model to surface the gaps in your source material before it drafts, which is where most SOPs go wrong silently. If you want a deeper intro to role-prompting as a discipline, our 9 tips for better Claude prompts walks through the pattern in detail.

Template 2 — The Existing-Draft Rewrite

Use this when there is a rough existing document — usually inconsistent, often out of date — and you need it rewritten into a standard format without losing the operational detail it already has. The trick is treating the draft as source material, not as the deliverable.

Role: You are an operations writer rewriting an inconsistent rough
draft into a standard SOP format. You preserve all operational
detail; you only fix structure, grammar, and clarity.

Objective: Rewrite the draft below into a complete SOP that matches
the template in Template 1, with no information loss.

List of steps (existing draft — paste below):
---
[PASTE EXISTING DRAFT]
---

Exceptions: After your rewrite, output a "lost-information audit"
table with two columns:
| Original sentence | Where it landed in the new SOP |
If a detail from the original does not appear in the new draft,
flag it explicitly. Do not silently drop information.

Source: Same structure as Template 1, but prioritise preserving the
order of operations from the original. Reorder only when the
original ordering is unsafe or clearly wrong.

The lost-information audit is what catches the 10% of operational detail that gets lost in any rewrite. A 2026 study of operations teams found that AI-assisted SOP drafting saves about 80% of writing time — but only when the rewrite prompt includes an explicit audit step. Without it, teams spend the saved time hunting for lost details.

Template 3 — The Decision-Tree Extract

Use this when the procedure has a clear branch point — a triage step, an approval gate, a "yes/no" routing decision — and the source material describes both paths but not the routing logic. The output is a single decision tree the model draws in Mermaid, which you can paste into Notion, Confluence, or a wiki.

Role: You are an operations analyst extracting decision logic from
a process description.

Objective: Convert the branch point described below into a Mermaid
flowchart I can paste into our documentation wiki.

List of steps (paste the relevant section of the SOP or process
description here):
---
[PASTE SECTION]
---

Exceptions: For each decision point, list:
- The question being asked
- The "yes" path (next step)
- The "no" path (next step)
- The escalation path (what to do if neither)

Source: Mermaid `graph TD` syntax. Use short node labels (under
6 words). No prose preamble. Output only the Mermaid code block.

Mermaid renders natively in GitHub, GitLab, Notion, Confluence, and most modern wikis — which is why it has become the de-facto format for AI-generated process diagrams. A well-formed Mermaid diagram is auditable in a way a hand-drawn flowchart is not, and operators can paste it into a runbook without redrawing.

Template 4 — The Exception-Handling Pass

Use this last, once the main SOP is drafted. The goal is to force the model to think through the realistic failure modes — the things that go wrong on a Tuesday at 4pm, not the things that go wrong in a compliance audit. This is the prompt that separates a "shelf document" from a working SOP.

Role: You are a senior operations lead stress-testing an SOP for
real-world failure modes.

Objective: For each numbered step in the SOP below, generate the
2-3 most likely failure modes an operator will hit in the first
30 days of using the document.

List of steps (paste the SOP):
---
[PASTE SOP]
---

Exceptions: For each failure mode, output:
- The step that fails (e.g. "Step 4")
- The failure (what goes wrong)
- The detection signal (how the operator knows)
- The recovery action (what to do)
- The escalation contact (role, not name)

Source: Markdown table. Columns: Step, Failure, Signal, Recovery,
Escalation. No prose preamble.

For a side-by-side look at how Claude, ChatGPT, and Gemini handle each of these four templates under load, our Claude vs GPT-5 vs Gemini prompting guide is the natural next read.

3 Mistakes to Avoid

1. Asking the model to draft without source material. "Write me an SOP for refund processing" produces a generic template that looks correct and contains zero operational detail. Always feed the model source material — a voice transcript, a rough draft, or bullet points. The most common failure mode is "the model invents steps that match industry norms but not our actual process," and the only fix is real input first.

2. Skipping the exception pass. The first draft covers the happy path. Real operations live on the exceptions — the missing input, the failed step, the escalated case. The Template 4 exception pass is what turns the SOP from a compliance document into a working tool. Skipping it is the single biggest reason AI-generated SOPs end up rewritten by hand.

3. Writing for auditors instead of operators. A common failure mode documented in process-writing guides: SOPs are written to satisfy a compliance review, not to help the person performing the work. Audit-oriented SOPs are full of jargon, cross-references, and legal-sounding language that obscures the actual steps. The result is a document that passes an audit and sits unused on a shelf. Write for the operator first, and the audit will pass on its own.

Putting It All Together

The full R.O.L.E.S. pipeline looks like this:

  1. Blank-Slate Draft — turn a voice transcript or bullet notes into a first draft.
  2. Existing-Draft Rewrite — normalise a rough document into standard format, with a lost-information audit.
  3. Decision-Tree Extract — convert branch points into a Mermaid flowchart for the wiki.
  4. Exception Pass — stress-test the SOP against realistic failure modes.

For most operational processes, all four steps run in a single chat session and take under 45 minutes. The exception pass is the part that makes the document actually usable. That is what makes a prompt the best prompt for SOP work — not cleverness, but structure. If you want a longer structured-document application, our best prompt for business plan guide uses the same framework.

Related: Best Prompt for Business Plan — the same R.O.L.E.S. framework applied to a longer structured document, with a 7-section template and the 3 mistakes that flatten an AI-generated plan.
Related: 9 Tips to Write Better Claude Prompts — the role-priming pattern in Template 1 comes straight from this guide, with 9 specific techniques for Claude 4.7.
Related: Best Prompt for Product Description — a focused 5-part prompt for ecommerce copy that uses the same structured-prompt thinking on a much shorter deliverable.

Best Prompt for Sales: Cold Outreach That Books Meetings

Ask ChatGPT or Claude to "write me a sales email" and you get the same beige corporate draft every rep on the planet gets: "I hope this email finds you well. I wanted to reach out regarding..." That template trains the recipient to delete the message before line two. The reason is not the model — it is the prompt. A vague prompt hands the AI every creative decision, and the AI defaults to the median sales email it learned from millions of training messages. A structured prompt hands the AI only the decisions that matter — who is this person, what do they care about, what is the specific ask — and the result is an email that sounds like a human wrote it for that one specific reader.

This guide is the structured version. Five copy-paste best prompt for sales templates for the five emails that drive B2B pipeline: cold outreach, follow-up, discovery call prep, proposal, and re-engagement. Each template is built on the 5-part prompt structure from our general prompt engineering guide, tested across ChatGPT, Claude, and Gemini, and tuned with the model-specific tweaks from our Claude vs GPT-5 vs Gemini comparison. For the underlying writing mechanics, see AI Prompt for Email Writing: 5 Templates That Get Replies. For the marketing-angled version — landing pages, ad copy, social — see Best Prompt for Marketing: 7 Templates That Convert in 2026. For the LinkedIn-specific outbound play, see Best Prompt for LinkedIn: Connection Requests That Don't Feel Like Spam.

Why "Write Me a Sales Email" Is Not a Prompt

Sales email is the highest-context task you can throw at an LLM. The model has to infer the relationship between sender and recipient, the prior history, the buying stage, the desired outcome, the right tone, the right length, and the right level of formality — all from a one-line instruction. The default behavior is "business professional, neutral, generic," which is exactly the voice that gets archived. The fix is the same as it is for any other prompt: name the output, define the constraints, give an example, and tell the model what success looks like.

The numbers back this up. Saleshandy's analysis of 100M+ cold emails found the average reply rate is 3–5%, while top performers hit 8–10%+. Built for B2B's 10,000-campaign study found a tighter spread: average 1–3%, top 10% hitting 8–12%. The difference is not the product, the price, or the market — it is the message. PhantomBuster's research on ChatGPT prompts for sales emails makes the same point: the most effective prompts use personalization tokens specifically to make the CTA specific to the recipient, and they front-load the trigger event in the first 8 words.

There is one more thing to internalize before the templates. Reply rate is not the same as meeting rate. An email can get a 10% reply rate and a 0.5% meeting rate if the ask is unclear, the next step is missing, or the subject promised something the body did not deliver. Every prompt below is built to optimize for meeting, not reply — which means a clear CTA, a low-friction next step, and a reason to respond that is not "just wanted to follow up." The first follow-up email in a cold sequence can lift reply rates by 49–66% over a single send — and the difference between a follow-up that gets a meeting and one that gets ignored is whether the second email adds new context, not whether it is "just checking in."

The 5-Part Sales Prompt Framework

Before the templates, here is the universal structure. Every sales prompt should fill in all five parts. If you can only fill in three, the model will fill in the rest — and the default is the kind of email that gets archived.

  1. Role & Recipient — who is selling, who is buying, and what is the relationship. "I am a B2B SaaS rep writing to a VP of Sales at a 50-100 person company" is a role. "I am writing a sales email" is not.
  2. Context & Trigger — what just happened, or what specific thing about the prospect triggered this outreach. A trigger event (new funding, a job change, a published post, a tech-stack change detected by tools like Clay or Apollo) is the single biggest lever on reply rate.
  3. Value Proposition — what is in it for the recipient, in one sentence. Not a feature list, not a company bio. A specific outcome: cut sales email drafting time by 70%, lift reply rates from 4% to 11%, save 6 hours per rep per week.
  4. Ask / CTA — the specific, low-friction next step. "Open to a 15-minute call next Wednesday?" beats "let me know if you want to chat." A calendar link beats "find time on my calendar." A specific yes/no question beats an open-ended one.
  5. Format & Tone Constraints — length (under 80 words for cold email, 50-75 is the proven sweet spot, 40-60 for follow-up), tone (casual, peer-to-peer, no "I hope this finds you well"), signoff, and any specific phrases to avoid.
💡 The trigger event rule: Parts 1, 2, and 3 must be specific to this recipient. If you can swap the recipient name and the email still works, it is too generic. Built for B2B's data found that 50-75 word emails get a 12% response rate; 200+ word emails get 2%. Length is not just style — it is the difference between a meeting and the trash folder.

That is the framework. The next five sections are the templates — one for each of the five sales emails that drive pipeline.

1. Cold Outreach Prompt

The hardest sales email to write is the first one. The prospect did not ask for it, does not know you, and has 200 other unread messages. The job of a cold sales email is not to close a deal — it is to earn a 6-line reply. Here is the prompt:

You are a B2B sales rep at a SaaS company. Write a cold outreach email under 80 words.

ROLE: I am writing to a [job title] at a [company size] company in [industry].
TRIGGER: They [specific trigger event — e.g., "just announced a Series B led by Accel" / "published a post on reducing sales email drafting time" / "hired their first RevOps lead last month" / "switched from HubSpot to Salesforce this quarter"].
VALUE: We help companies like [reference customer] [specific outcome — e.g., "cut sales email drafting time by 70% while lifting reply rates 2x"]. One specific result: [reference customer] went from 4% to 11% reply rate in 6 weeks.
CTA: Open to a 15-minute call next week? I will send a specific agenda, not a generic demo.
TONE: Peer-to-peer, no fluff, no "I hope this finds you well." No exclamation marks.
SIGN-OFF: [Your name], [one-line title]

Example Output

Subject: 11% reply rate in 6 weeks

Hi Sarah,

Saw you just hired your first RevOps lead at Northwind — congrats. Most teams in that window spend 6-8 hours/week drafting outbound that converts at 3-4%.

We helped Replicant (similar profile, 60 reps) go from 4% to 11% reply rate in 6 weeks, while cutting drafting time by 70%. The short version: structured prompts + a sequenced follow-up framework, not magic.

Open to a 15-minute call next week? I will send a specific agenda — what to do in the first 30 days, what to skip.

Best,
Alex Chen
Head of Outbound, Acme

P.S. The Replicant teardown is here if you want the data first: [link]
🚀 The follow-up move: The first follow-up should add new context, not just "bump." Instantly's research on 100M+ cold emails found that 55% of replies to cold email campaigns come from a follow-up email, not the initial message. The 3-7-7 cadence (Day 0, Day 3, Day 10, Day 17) captures 93% of total replies by Day 10.

2. Follow-Up Prompt (Bump with Value)

Most follow-ups fail because they are "just checking in." The prospect's inbox has 30 other "just checking in" messages. The follow-up that gets a reply is the one that adds a new piece of context, a new data point, or a new reason to respond. Here is the prompt:

You are a B2B sales rep following up on a cold outreach that received no reply. Write a follow-up under 60 words.

ORIGINAL EMAIL: [paste the first email you sent]
DAYS SINCE: [3-5 days is the proven range]
NEW CONTEXT: [pick ONE: a relevant case study, a new trigger event, a short relevant insight, a question that reframes the original ask]
TONE: Even shorter and lower-friction than the first email. Assume the recipient is busy and skeptical. No "just wanted to bump this up" or "circling back."
CTA: Make the ask smaller. "Worth a 10-minute look?" beats "open to a 15-minute call?" A specific yes/no question beats an open-ended one.

Example Output

Subject: Re: 11% reply rate in 6 weeks

Hi Sarah,

One more data point since my last note — we just published the Replicant teardown with the exact prompt templates and sequencing. Took them from 4% to 11% in 6 weeks.

Worth a 10-minute look? If it's not a fit, I'll stop the sequence.

Best,
Alex

The pattern: new context (the teardown), smaller ask (10 minutes vs. 15), explicit out (stop the sequence). That last line is the most underrated move in cold sales email — giving the recipient permission to say no. It paradoxically raises reply rates because it signals confidence and reduces the perceived cost of responding. Stormy AI's research on the 2026 "beige email crisis" agrees: in a world where every rep has access to ChatGPT, the emails that get replies are the ones that add a piece of information the prospect could not have generated themselves.

3. Discovery Call Prep Prompt

Most reps show up to discovery calls unprepared, and the call becomes a generic "tell me about your business" demo. The fix is to use AI to generate a one-page call brief in 5 minutes — prospect background, likely pain points, smart questions to ask, and red flags to watch for. The prompt:

You are a B2B sales rep preparing for a 30-minute discovery call. Generate a call brief under 400 words.

PROSPECT: [company name], [industry], [employee count], [recent trigger event if any]
MY PRODUCT: [one sentence — what you sell and the specific outcome you deliver]
CALL GOAL: [book a demo / scope a pilot / get a referral / qualify out]

Generate these sections:
1. PROSPECT SNAPSHOT (3-4 bullets): What they do, who they sell to, what just changed that triggered this call.
2. LIKELY PAIN POINTS (3 bullets): The 3 problems a [job title] at a [company size] in [industry] is most likely trying to solve right now.
3. DISCOVERY QUESTIONS (5 questions): Open-ended questions that uncover pain, not yes/no questions. Include one question about cost of inaction.
4. RED FLAGS (2-3 bullets): Signs the prospect is not a real opportunity — wrong ICP, no budget authority, shopping for a price comparison, etc.
5. NEXT STEP PROPOSAL: The specific ask to close with, with a specific date and a specific agenda for the next meeting.

The structure does three things. It forces you to think about the prospect before the call (not during it). It generates questions that surface pain instead of confirming what you already believe. And it pre-commits you to a specific next step, which is the difference between a discovery call that books a demo and one that ends with "let me think about it and get back to you." Clay's research on AI-native SDR teams found that reps using AI-generated call briefs book 23% more second meetings than reps who wing it, because the brief forces a specific next-step ask in advance.

4. Sales Proposal Email Prompt

Sales proposal emails fail for a predictable reason: they summarize the proposal instead of selling the decision. The decision-maker does not need a recap of the deck — they need a reason to act this week instead of next quarter. The prompt:

You are an account executive sending a post-demo proposal email. Write under 250 words.

RECIPIENT: [decision-maker role and title — the person who signs, not the user]
WHAT WE AGREED IN THE DEMO: [2-3 specific points — their pain, the use case they want to solve first, the success metric they named]
PROPOSAL SUMMARY: [3 bullet points — what they get in phase 1, what it costs, what the outcome looks like in 90 days]
RISK OF INACTION: [one sentence — what happens if they wait another quarter]
CTA: Specific next step with a specific date. "Sign by Friday to start the kickoff the following Monday" beats "Let me know what you think."
TONE: Confident, direct, no "I hope this proposal meets your needs." Treat the proposal as a done deal waiting for a signature, not a request for approval.
💡 The "risk of inaction" line: Most sales proposal emails bury the cost of doing nothing. The line that converts stalled proposals is the one that names the specific thing that will not happen if they wait — a missed quarter, a delayed launch, a competitor's announcement they will not be ready for. The risk has to be specific to their situation, not generic FOMO.

5. Re-Engagement / Breakup Email Prompt

Re-engagement emails are the most underused sales email type. After 3-4 follow-ups with no reply, the default move is to mark the lead as "not interested" and move on. The smarter move is a breakup email that re-opens the conversation with low pressure and an explicit out. The prompt:

You are a B2B sales rep sending a breakup email to a prospect who has not replied to 3+ follow-ups. Write under 80 words.

ORIGINAL OUTREACH: [paste the first email you sent or a one-line summary of the offer]
LAST TOUCH: [date and content of the last email — usually a bump with new context]
RE-ENGAGEMENT HOOK: [pick ONE: a relevant trigger event from the past 30 days, a closing-the-loop case study, a simple "is this still a priority?" question]
PERMISSION-TO-CLOSE-OUT LINE: [one sentence — "If the timing is off, just reply 'not now' and I'll close the file and stop the sequence."]

TONE: No pressure, no "just checking in," no "circling back." Assume the prospect is busy, possibly on parental leave, possibly changed roles, possibly just not a fit right now. The goal is to either re-open the conversation OR get a clean "not now" so you can stop wasting their inbox.
SIGN-OFF: First name only. Lower the formality.

The pattern: acknowledge the silence, give a reason to re-engage (or not), and make the no-reply path explicit. The permission-to-close-out line is the most important piece — it converts a passive "delete" into an active "reply not now" or "actually yes, let's talk." Built for B2B's research on the 3-7-7 cadence found that breakup emails re-open 8-12% of "dead" conversations, which is a higher meeting rate than the original cold outreach. The cost of the email is zero; the upside of one re-opened conversation is the entire year's quota.

3 Sales Prompt Mistakes to Avoid

Even with great templates, three patterns consistently produce bad sales emails. Catch them before you send.

Mistake 1: "I Hope This Email Finds You Well"

This phrase is the most-deleted opening line in sales email history. The prospect knows you do not hope that. The prospect knows you copy-pasted the line. The prospect has read it 200 times this week. Open with the specific trigger event, not with a wish for their wellbeing. "Saw you just hired your first RevOps lead at Northwind" is a better opening. "Saw you just hired your first RevOps lead" is even better — the prospect's eyes will go to the next line to find out how you know that. Teamgate's research on AI-driven sales development calls this "shallow personalization" — the kind of personalization that signals you did 30 seconds of research, not 3 minutes. The fix is the trigger event: name something specific the prospect just did, and the rest of the email earns the right to exist.

Mistake 2: Burying the Ask

The biggest reply-rate killer is the sales email that ends with "let me know if you have any questions" instead of a specific ask. "Any questions" is a question the prospect can answer with no action. A specific ask — "open to a 15-minute call next Wednesday at 2pm PT?" — requires a yes or a no. The easier the response, the more likely it is to happen. Built for B2B's data on email length found that 50-75 word emails convert at 12%, 125-200 word emails at 7%, and 200+ word emails at 2%. The 200+ word emails almost always end with a buried ask. The fix is to make the ask the last sentence of the email, in bold, with a specific date.

Mistake 3: Token Personalization Instead of Trigger Personalization

The third mistake is the most subtle. "Token personalization" is inserting {First Name}, {Company Name}, and {Industry} into a template. "Trigger personalization" is naming a specific thing the prospect just did that connects to your value prop. The first signals "I ran your name through a tool." The second signals "I read your LinkedIn post, looked at your job change, and noticed a pattern that my product solves." BizKonnect's research on AI-driven B2B content found that contextual personalization (trigger-based) outperforms token personalization by 2-3x on reply rate, because the recipient can tell the difference. The fix is to spend 3-5 minutes on prospect research before writing the email, and feed the AI one specific trigger event per email. The trigger is the hook. Without it, the email is noise.
