Best Prompt for LinkedIn: Posts That Get 10K Impressions

Why most LinkedIn posts die in 2 lines

You wrote a LinkedIn post you were proud of. It stalled at 47 impressions with three likes from your mom. Welcome to the 2026 algorithm: it's not punishing you for being boring, it's punishing you for being generic. The new ranker rewards Depth Score — dwell time, saves, private shares, substantive comment threads. That's exactly what the best prompt for LinkedIn fixes: it forces every part of your post to earn its keep. Below: 5 copy-paste templates for the highest-impact formats (founders, B2B sales, job seekers, personal brand, carousel).

The 5-part structure behind every high-reach LinkedIn post

Every LinkedIn post that pulls real reach in 2026 has the same 5 moves:

  1. Hook (first 2 lines): the only lines before "see more". Concrete, specific, a little uncomfortable — a number, a confession, a contrarian take.
  2. Empty line: forces a visual pause — what the dwell-time signal needs.
  3. Story (1 short paragraph): a specific moment — what happened, who was there, what you decided. Vague claims don't earn dwell time; specific moments do.
  4. Lesson (1 short paragraph): the takeaway people save. State it as a principle, not a platitude.
  5. CTA (last line): a specific, low-friction question. Not "what do you think?" — that's the most-skipped CTA on LinkedIn.

Template 1: Founder / operator post (the "ugly lesson" pattern)

This is the format that took most of the "build-in-public" set to 100K+ followers. The trick: the post isn't about the win — it's about the part that almost killed the company.

You are a ghostwriter for a founder posting on LinkedIn. Write a post
that uses the ugly-lesson structure: hook with a specific failure number,
then the moment, then the reframe, then a question.

HOOK (first 2 lines, must stop the scroll):
- Lead with a specific number, dollar amount, or concrete failure
- Avoid clichés: no "I almost gave up", no "they laughed at me"
- One option: "We lost $47,000 in 11 days. Here's the second decision
  that lost us another $18,000."
- Another option: "3 weeks ago I fired our best engineer. Today I'm
  glad I did. Here's the part I didn't expect."

STORY (1 short paragraph, max 4 sentences):
- Set the scene in 1 sentence (when, who, what)
- Describe the specific moment of the failure (the call, the email,
  the conversation)
- End with the realization that changed how you operate

LESSON (1 short paragraph):
- State the principle as a single bold sentence first
- Then 1 sentence of context for why it generalizes
- Example: "Speed is not a strategy. The thing you skip to go faster
  is usually the thing that would have saved you."

CTA (last line, specific question):
- "What's the decision you've been speeding up that actually deserved
  a slowdown?"
- "Which of your last 3 'urgent' decisions would have waited a week?"

OUTPUT:
- 150-220 words total
- Single line breaks every 1-2 sentences (no walls of text)
- NO hashtags in the body (LinkedIn's 2026 algorithm deprioritizes them)
- NO external links in the post body (drops reach by ~60%)
- 1 emoji max, used only in the CTA line if at all

When to use it: weekly founder updates, milestone posts, post-mortems after a lost deal, hiring/team changes. Works best when posted Tuesday–Thursday between 8–10am US Eastern.

Template 2: B2B sales / outbound LinkedIn post

B2B sales posts are explicitly down-weighted by the 2026 algorithm if they promote your product. The escape hatch: post about the buyer's problem instead. Reads like thought leadership but primes inbound.

Write a LinkedIn post for a B2B sales rep or founder. The post must
teach one specific thing about the buyer's world without mentioning
the seller's product.

STEP 1 — Pick the buyer:
- Be specific: "VP of Sales at a 50-200 person SaaS company hiring
  their first RevOps hire" — NOT "B2B leaders" or "sales teams"

STEP 2 — Identify the unspoken pain (the thing buyers won't say in
the first call):
- Example: "They bought a tool 6 months ago nobody uses"
- Example: "Their champion left and the eval started over"
- Example: "Their CFO wants a 2× ROI case before any new vendor"

STEP 3 — Write the hook as the buyer's private thought:
- "Every RevOps leader I talk to admits the same thing in the second
  call: their CRM is wrong. Not the data — the structure."
- "If you're a VP Sales hiring your first RevOps person this quarter,
  the worst thing you can optimize for is technical skill."

STEP 4 — Story:
- Describe a specific call you had (no name, no company)
- Quote the buyer directly: "She said: 'I don't need another dashboard,
  I need to know which 3 deals in my pipeline are real.'"
- Show what you told them to do

STEP 5 — Reframe:
- Name the principle behind your advice as a one-liner
- Example: "The job of a first RevOps hire is to delete things,
  not build them."

STEP 6 — CTA (specific to the buyer):
- "If you're hiring your first RevOps person this quarter, what's the
  one thing you'd want them to delete in their first 30 days?"

WORD COUNT: 200-250 words
NO product mentions, NO "DM me", NO booking links in body
OUTPUT 3 versions so the rep can A/B test the hook

When to use it: 2–3 times per week as a sales rep or founder who sells B2B. Pairs with daily commenting on 3–5 ICP posts (the algorithm compounding move).

Template 3: Job seeker / career-pivot post

Job-seeker posts are treated as low-distribution by the algorithm. The ones that do break through aren't about "I'm looking for a role" — they're about a sharp, hire-able observation from the field.

Write a LinkedIn post for a job seeker or career changer. The post
must demonstrate hire-able thinking without saying "I'm looking for
a job."

CONSTRAINT: Do NOT open with "I'm excited to announce" or "After X
years, I'm thrilled to share" — these are the most-skipped openings
on LinkedIn.

HOOK — Start with a sharp observation from the work:
- "I've interviewed 23 candidates for senior PM roles this quarter.
  4 of them could tell me what they shipped that broke and what they
  learned from it. Here's what those 4 had in common."
- "Most career changers optimize for the wrong variable. The thing
  hiring managers actually want to see isn't transferability — it's
  evidence of fast learning in a specific domain."

STRUCTURE:
1. Hook (1-2 lines, sharp observation)
2. Empty line (forces the visual pause)
3. The pattern you noticed (1 paragraph, 3-5 sentences)
4. The 2-3 specific things the best candidates did differently
5. The reframe (what this means for the reader)
6. CTA — question aimed at fellow job seekers, not at recruiters:
   "What's the strongest piece of evidence you've put in front of a
   hiring manager that wasn't on your resume?"

OUTPUT RULES:
- 180-230 words
- No "open to work" green frame photo (signals low-distribution to
  the algorithm)
- No "like this if you agree" engagement bait
- No hashtags except 0-1 niche tag in the comments

When to use it: once a week during a job search, paired with substantive comments on hiring managers' posts (the highest-leverage job-search motion in 2026).

Template 4: Personal-brand / thought-leadership post

This format builds authority over 6–12 months. The catch: 95% of "contrarian" posts on LinkedIn aren't actually contrarian — they restate consensus in a more aggressive tone. This prompt forces a real disagreement with prevailing wisdom.

Write a LinkedIn post that takes a real contrarian position in the
user's field. The position must (a) be specific enough that an
expert could disagree, and (b) be defensible with one concrete example.

BRAINSTORM (write 3 possible contrarian takes first, then pick the
sharpest):
- Position 1: "X is overrated because [specific reason]"
- Position 2: "The standard advice about Y is wrong for [specific
  audience] because [specific reason]"
- Position 3: "Most people in [field] do Z. The opposite — W —
  works better because [specific reason]"

PICK THE SHARPEST ONE AND WRITE THE POST:

HOOK:
- State the contrarian position in the first line, no hedging
- Format: "Unpopular opinion in [field]: [position]."
- OR: "I've spent 5 years in [field] and I'm going to stop pretending
  [common practice] works."

EMPTY LINE

WHY MOST PEOPLE DISAGREE (1 paragraph):
- Steel-man the conventional view in 2 sentences
- "I get why most [practitioners] think this. [Conventional logic]."
- This step is what separates contrarian posts from troll posts.

WHAT I'VE SEEN INSTEAD (1-2 paragraphs):
- 1-2 specific examples (anonymized clients, your own work, observed
  outcomes)
- Use concrete numbers where possible

THE REFRAME (1 paragraph):
- State the principle behind your contrarian view as a single
  sentence
- Explain who this advice is for and who it isn't

CTA:
- "If you've tried [conventional approach] and it worked, I'd genuinely
  love to hear what made it work for you. I'm collecting counter-examples."

OUTPUT: 220-280 words
TONE: Direct, not aggressive. Confident, not arrogant.
NO emojis in body except 0-1 in the CTA

When to use it: 1× per week, paired with daily commenting on 5 ICP posts. This is the slow-compounding format — the audience builds 6–12 months in.

Template 5: Carousel / document post (the 6.60% engagement format)

Document carousels lead LinkedIn formats at 6.60% engagement vs ~2% text-only (dataslayer, 2026). The reason: dwell time — a 10-slide carousel holds a reader 60+ seconds, the algorithm's threshold. This prompt generates the slide breakdown and post body in one go.

You are a content designer who specializes in LinkedIn document carousels.
Generate a 10-slide carousel on the topic provided, plus a LinkedIn post
to publish it with.

SLIDE STRUCTURE (always this layout):
- Slide 1: Cover slide. Title in 5-8 words. Subtitle in 10-15 words
  that promises a specific outcome. NO "click here", NO emoji-as-decoration.
- Slide 2: The problem. State the gap between what most people do and
  what works. 30-50 words.
- Slide 3-8: One principle per slide. Each slide = one bold headline (3-6
  words) + 2-3 lines of explanation + 1 specific example or number.
- Slide 9: The summary. List the 5-7 takeaways as a numbered list,
  each on its own line.
- Slide 10: CTA slide. "If this was useful: follow [Name] for [topic]
  posts. Save this for later. Repost if it helped you."

VISUAL RULES:
- Use the same accent color for every slide (recommend: dark blue,
  off-white background, single accent color)
- Maximum 6 lines of text per slide
- Use icons or simple diagrams, not stock photos
- Headline font 2× larger than body font

POST BODY (publishes WITH the carousel):
- Hook (first 2 lines): specific, surprising, sets up the carousel
- Empty line
- 2 sentences: why you made this and who it's for
- The 1-line promise of the carousel: "10 slides on [topic]. The
  framework on slide 4 is what changed my [year/revenue/results]."
- Empty line
- "Save it for the next time you [trigger moment]."
- Empty line
- CTA question: "Which slide are you going to try first?"

WORD COUNT: post body 80-130 words (carousel does the heavy lifting)
FILE FORMAT: PDF, exported at 1080×1350 px (4:5 portrait)

When to use it: 1–2× per month. Highest reach-per-post of any format but takes 2–4 hours to design well. Best for frameworks, lists, and data-heavy content.

The 3 mistakes that kill reach on every format

These 3 mistakes account for ~80% of the "I posted and nothing happened" complaints.

Mistake 1: External link in the post body. LinkedIn's 2026 algorithm cuts reach ~60% for posts with an outbound link — and the first-comment workaround is also penalized as of early 2026. Post natively; mention the link in comments after you've earned the reach.

Mistake 2: The hashtag sandwich. Posts closing with 5–10 hashtags get down-ranked — the ranker treats hashtag density as a low-quality signal. Use 0–1 niche hashtags in body, extras in comments.

Mistake 3: Daily low-effort posts instead of weekly substantive ones. The algorithm rewards topic focus over volume. The pattern that works: one substantive weekly post + 3–5 daily substantive comments (30–80 words each, extending the argument). Combined effect: 5–10× the reach of someone posting daily without commenting.

Putting it all together: a weekly posting rhythm

The best prompt for LinkedIn is the one you actually use. A sustainable weekly rhythm:

  • Monday: 30 min commenting on 3–5 ICP posts (highest-ROI move in 2026).
  • Tue/Wed, 8–10am US Eastern: one substantive post using Templates 1, 3, or 4.
  • Thursday: 15 min replying to every comment within 24h.
  • One weekend a month: one carousel using Template 5 (4-hour batch).
  • Skip Friday/Sunday: lowest B2B engagement windows.

Run this rhythm for 8 weeks — one post per week, daily comments on ICP posts, one carousel per month is what turns 400 connections into 5,000 followers and a steady inbound channel. Pick the template that fits your current goal, ship your first post this week, and don't skip the commenting hours — they're doing more work than the post itself.

Best Prompt for Productivity: 10 Daily AI Workflows

10 copy-paste prompt workflows for the 10 things you do every day: email, calendar, todos, notes, research, and more.

If you use ChatGPT, Claude, or Gemini the same way every morning — open the tab, type a quick question, copy the answer, close the tab — you are leaving hours on the table. The best prompt for productivity is not a single magic sentence. It is a small library of structured prompts you can paste in five seconds, each one tuned to a recurring workflow: inbox triage, calendar planning, meeting notes, research, weekly review, and shutdown. This guide gives you exactly that: ten copy-paste productivity prompt templates, organized by the part of your day they replace, plus the small adjustments that turn generic output into something you can actually ship.

Why a Library Beats One Big Prompt

Most "ultimate productivity prompt" articles give you a single 200-word monstrosity you are supposed to paste every morning. That fails for two reasons. First, the AI loses focus: by the third instruction in a giant system prompt, the model is already hedging and the output starts reading like a first draft of nothing. Second, your day is not one task. Your day is fifteen small tasks chained together, and the AI does each one better when it has a clean context window and a sharp role for that one task.

The right pattern is a small library of short, role-pinned prompts — each one doing one job well — plus a fast way to swap them in. McKinsey's 2024 workplace survey found that teams using AI for routine work saved an average of 30 to 40 percent of the time on those tasks, but only when the prompts were templated and reused, not improvised every morning. The templates below are designed to be saved in a notes app, named clearly, and pulled up in seconds.

The 10 Daily AI Workflows

Each prompt below follows the same three-part recipe: Role + Raw Material + Output Shape. The role primes the model to think like a chief of staff or executive assistant. The raw material is your actual inputs — your inbox snippets, your meeting notes, your to-do list — not a hypothetical. The output shape pins down exactly what good looks like: a triaged table, a five-bullet summary, a one-page weekly review. Replace the bracketed sections with your real details and paste.

1. Morning Inbox Triage (saves 20–30 min)

You are a calm, fast executive assistant who has triaged thousands of inboxes. I will paste a batch of unread emails below. Sort them so I can clear my inbox in 15 minutes.

For each email return:
- SENDER (name)
- ONE-LINE SUBJECT
- ACTION: Reply / Read / Archive / Defer / Delete
- IF REPLY: a 1-sentence draft response

My role: [YOUR ROLE]
Today's priorities: [YOUR TOP 3]

EMAILS:
[PASTE INBOX SNIPPETS]

Why it works: the role pins the tone, the action taxonomy forces a decision on every email, and the optional draft reply means the only thing left for you to do is press send. If you want a deeper look at how summarization-style prompts stack up, see our summarization prompt guide.

2. Daily Plan From a Scattered To-Do List

You are a productivity coach trained in time-blocking and the Eisenhower Matrix. I will paste my raw to-do list. Convert it into a realistic 9-to-5 schedule for today.

Constraints:
- Group similar tasks (batching)
- Reserve two 90-minute deep-work blocks before noon
- Add a 15-minute buffer between meetings
- Flag any task that cannot fit today and propose where to move it

Format as a time-blocked table: TIME | TASK | NOTES

TODO LIST:
[PASTE]

The hardest part of any AI workflow like this is not the schedule itself — it is the realism. Telling the model to reserve deep-work blocks and to flag overflow is what stops you from getting a fantasy calendar that falls apart at 10:14 a.m.

3. Meeting Prep in 5 Minutes

You are my chief of staff preparing me for a meeting. I will paste the meeting title, attendees, and any pre-reads. Produce a one-page brief I can read in 5 minutes.

Sections:
1. WHO IS IN THE ROOM (one line per attendee, their stake)
2. THE LIKELY DECISION we need to walk out with
3. 3 OPEN QUESTIONS I should ask
4. 2 THINGS I should NOT bring up
5. MY OPENING LINE (one sentence)

MEETING:
[PASTE TITLE + ATTENDEES + LINKS]

This pairs naturally with our meeting notes prompt: run this prep prompt before the meeting, then run the notes prompt after. Together they bookend the meeting and you barely have to think about it.

4. Research Digest From a Stack of Links

You are a research analyst who writes briefs for an executive who has 10 minutes. I will paste a list of article URLs and a one-line question. For each URL produce:
- 3 BULLET SUMMARY (the only three things I need to know)
- ONE-LINE TAKEAWAY (the "so what" for my work)
- TRUST SCORE: High / Medium / Low based on source quality

Question: [YOUR QUESTION]
URLs:
[PASTE]

For longer docs, paste the text directly instead of the URL. The trust-score column forces the model to hedge appropriately so you do not over-rely on a single weak source.

5. End-of-Day Shutdown and Tomorrow's Top 3

You are my chief of staff helping me shut down the day cleanly. I will paste a brain-dump of what happened today. Produce:

1. A 5-bullet recap of what actually got done (not what I planned)
2. Anything still open that needs a tomorrow owner
3. The TOP 3 priorities for tomorrow, ordered by leverage
4. One sentence: what I should NOT do tomorrow

BRAIN DUMP:
[PASTE]

Closing the laptop with a clean shutdown ritual is the single highest-leverage productivity habit, and a five-second prompt makes it stick.

The Three Mistakes Almost Everyone Makes

Most people who try these templates give up after a week. The reason is almost always one of three errors, all of which are easy to fix once you see them.

Mistake 1: Writing a topic instead of a task. "Help me with my inbox" is a topic. "Triage my inbox and return ACTION: Reply / Read / Archive / Defer / Delete for each email" is a task. A topic tells the model what to talk about; a task tells it what to do, in what format, and to what standard. If you can rewrite your prompt as a noun phrase, it is still a topic.

Mistake 2: Vague constraints instead of binary rules. "Keep it concise" is not a constraint — it is a suggestion the model interprets on its own terms. Replace it with "Maximum 150 words" or "Return exactly 5 bullets." Effective constraints are binary: either the output satisfies them or it does not. Our guide to the 9 best Claude prompts covers this in more depth.

Mistake 3: Skipping the role. Omitting a role does not mean the model uses no role — it means the model averages across every role it has ever seen. Telling it "you are a calm, fast executive assistant" collapses that average into one specific persona, which is what makes the output feel like draft work instead of filler.

How to Save and Reuse These Prompts

Prompts that live only in your chat history do not save time. Pick one notes app — Notion, Obsidian, Apple Notes, even a pinned Slack message to yourself — and store each template with a short, searchable name. When you sit down to start your day, the workflow should be: open notes, copy template, paste, fill the brackets, send. If the workflow takes more than 30 seconds, you will stop doing it by day four.

The compounding return is in workflows, not in one-off chats. The first time you run the inbox triage prompt you will save ten minutes. The twentieth time, you will have a refined prompt that knows your role, your top priorities, and your writing voice — and you will save twenty-five minutes without thinking about it.

• Best Prompt for Summarization — the sister skill to inbox triage.
• Best Prompt for Meeting Notes — pairs with the meeting prep prompt above.

Best Prompt for Negotiation: Get the Deal Without the Back-and-Forth

The 4-part prompt that turns a counter-offer email into a 3-step negotiation script with walk-away anchors.

Why Generic AI Prompts Fail at Negotiation

Most people walk into best prompt for negotiation with the wrong mental model. They type "help me negotiate a higher salary" into ChatGPT or Claude and get a polished email that sounds like a press release: confident, generic, and completely missing the leverage that actually closes a deal. The output reads well. It just doesn't move the number.

The problem is not the model. It is the input. A negotiation prompt needs four ingredients most people leave out: your real constraints, the other side's likely position, a specific ask with reasoning, and a walk-away anchor. Without those, the model fills the gaps with what it has read in 1,000 MBA case studies — which is exactly the language your counterpart has already heard from the last ten candidates.

Below is the same 4-part framework used by recruiters and procurement leads, packed into copy-paste templates for the four most common negotiation situations: salary, vendor renewals, scope-creep pushback, and partnership splits.

The 4-Part Negotiation Prompt Framework

Every prompt below follows the same skeleton, which you can adapt for any deal that walks through your inbox.

Part 1 — Context (who you are, what the deal is, what stage you are at): the model needs to know whether you are early in a salary conversation, mid-renewal, or already at a deadlock. Same model, very different answer.

Part 2 — The Ask (a specific number plus the reasoning behind it): always include both. "I want $95k" is an anchor without justification. "I want $95k because the median for this role in this city is $98k according to Levels.fyi and I am taking on a 20% scope increase" gives the model something to defend.

Part 3 — Constraints (deadline, alternative options, deal-breakers): this is what the model will use to set its walk-away. If you do not state it, the model will assume you are infinitely flexible, and the script will be.

Part 4 — Output Format (email / phone script / counter-message / talking points): telling the model the channel changes the word choice, sentence length, and how aggressive calibrated questions should sound.

Template 1: The Salary Counter-Offer Email

This is the one most people will copy. Use it after you have done 10 minutes of market research on Levels.fyi, Glassdoor, or a recruiter DM. The prompt bakes in objective criteria — the only thing that survives a recruiter's pushback.

You are an expert salary-negotiation coach. I just received a job offer and want to write a counter-offer email that is calm, data-backed, and leaves room for the recruiter to say yes.

CONTEXT:
- Role: [Job Title] at [Company Name]
- Location: [City, Country]
- Offer: $[X] base, [equity / bonus / benefits]
- My experience: [X years, 1-2 specific wins with numbers]

ASK:
I want to counter at $[target]. My reasoning:
- Levels.fyi median for this role in this city is $[Y]
- I am taking on a 20% scope increase from my last role
- [One more objective fact: competing offer, market shift, etc.]

CONSTRAINTS:
- Deadline: I need to respond by [date]
- Walk-away: below $[Z] I cannot accept
- I prefer a base increase but will trade for [equity / signing bonus / extra PTO / remote days]

OUTPUT:
- One counter-offer email, under 200 words
- Tone: warm, professional, no threats
- End with a calibrated question that invites a counter, not a yes/no
- Do not use phrases like "I am worth" or "I deserve" — anchor on data, not emotion

Read the email out loud before you send it. If any sentence makes you cringe, the recruiter will feel it too. If you can say every line without flinching, the model wrote you a usable script.

Template 2: The Vendor or SaaS Renewal

Vendor renewals are where companies leave the most money on the table, because nobody on the buying side has time to play hardball. The prompt below forces the model to act like a procurement lead willing to walk.

You are a senior procurement negotiator. I am up for renewal on [Vendor / SaaS name] and want to push for a 20-30% reduction without losing the features my team depends on.

CONTEXT:
- Current spend: $[X] / year
- Contract end: [date]
- Usage last 90 days: [down X%, stable, up X%]
- Features we actually use: [list 3-5]
- Features we never use: [list 3-5]

ASK:
I want to renew at $[target] with [same / reduced] features. Reasoning:
- [One objective fact: competitor pricing, market shift, internal budget cut]

CONSTRAINTS:
- I have 90 days to migrate if they refuse (this is true or false — tell me honestly)
- I can accept multi-year lock-in for a bigger discount
- I am willing to be a case-study customer in exchange for a free tier

OUTPUT:
- One renewal email under 200 words
- A 3-bullet "if they say no" follow-up
- One calibrated question to put the decision back on them

The "I have 90 days to migrate" line only works if it is true. If the vendor knows you are locked in, your walk-away is zero, and the model will detect that. Be honest in the prompt and the model will write a strategy you can actually execute.

Template 3: Client Scope-Creep Pushback

This prompt keeps the relationship intact while drawing a clean line on the extra work that quietly appeared after the SOW was signed.

You are a freelance strategist who values long-term client relationships. A client is asking for work outside the original scope, and I need to push back without sounding defensive.

CONTEXT:
- Original SOW: [summary, 1-2 lines]
- New ask: [what they want added]
- Estimated extra hours: [X]
- Relationship: [X months / years, X projects done]

ASK:
I want to politely refuse the extra work, OR offer it as paid add-on at $[rate]. Reasoning:
- My current workload is at 100% capacity
- The original SOW did not include this scope
- Saying yes sets a precedent I cannot sustain

CONSTRAINTS:
- I want to keep this client for at least [X] more projects
- I am open to a phased delivery if they have a hard deadline
- I will not work for free to "prove" the value

OUTPUT:
- One reply email under 180 words
- Two calibrated questions to clarify whether this is urgent or "nice to have"
- A short alternative offer they are likely to accept

Calibrated questions are the secret. "How am I supposed to do that within the original scope?" forces the client to solve your capacity problem for you. They will usually offer a deadline extension or pay for the extra hours — both of which you can live with.

Template 4: Partnership and Equity Splits

For a co-founder equity split, a JV, or a strategic partnership, you need a longer document. The prompt below is a one-shot briefing memo you can send to your lawyer or advisor before the next call.

You are a partnerships strategist. I am entering a [co-founder / JV / strategic partnership] discussion and need a one-page briefing memo before the negotiation call.

CONTEXT:
- Parties: [you, partner, role of each]
- Deal size: $[X] valuation, [X]% equity, or [X] revenue share
- What I bring: [list]
- What they bring: [list]
- Time horizon: [X months to first revenue / exit]

ASK:
I want a briefing memo with:
1. The 3 leverage points I have that the other side under-weights
2. The 3 leverage points they have that I under-weight
3. A recommended opening ask and a walk-away number
4. The 2-3 trades I should prepare (low-value-for-them / high-value-for-me)

CONSTRAINTS:
- I want to preserve the relationship for at least [X] years
- I am willing to accept a smaller share if it includes [board seat, veto rights, IP ownership]
- I will not sign anything with a [non-compete / exclusivity clause] longer than [X months]

OUTPUT:
- One-page memo in clean prose, no fluff
- A "do not say" list of 3 phrases that weaken my position
- A "must say" list of 3 phrases that anchor on objective criteria

Most amateurs say "I think we are both getting a fair deal" — which immediately concedes the framing. The model will flag these for you if you ask.

The Mistakes That Make AI Sound Recruiter-Oblivious

Recruiters and counterparts can spot bad AI instantly. The tells are generic enthusiasm, inflated confidence, fake market claims, and sentences no real person would say on a call. If your draft sounds over-polished and under-informed, the other side will feel it. Here are the four mistakes to avoid, and how the prompt above neutralizes each one.

Mistake 1 — Asking the model to "be aggressive." Aggression reads as insecurity. The templates above use calibrated questions instead, which put the decision back on the other side without raising the temperature.

Mistake 2 — Hiding your constraints. If you have a deadline, a relocation issue, a visa concern, or a limited appetite to walk away, include it. Good prompts make the model more honest and more strategic. Bad prompts turn the model into a bluff generator — and bluffing without a walk-away is the fastest way to lose.

Mistake 3 — Feeding the model fake data. Do not invent competing offers, made-up achievements, or salary numbers you have not verified. A counterpart will check, and the negotiation collapses the moment they find out.

Mistake 4 — Trusting the first draft. The output is a starting point, not a finished script. Spend five minutes editing for your actual voice. If you cannot say a sentence out loud without cringing, rewrite it. The prompt gets you 80% of the way; your voice gets the last 20% that closes the deal.

A 10-Minute Pre-Send Checklist

Before you hit send on any AI-assisted negotiation message, run through this list. It is short on purpose — the goal is to keep the deal moving, not to add bureaucracy.

  • Did I include a specific number plus the reasoning behind it?
  • Did I state my walk-away clearly in the prompt (and to myself)?
  • Is every claim I am making something I can defend if asked?
  • Does the email end with a calibrated question, not a yes/no?
  • Can I read every sentence out loud without cringing?

If all five boxes are checked, send it. If even one is missing, give the prompt another pass — or read our full guide on 9 tips for better Claude prompts and prompt engineering fundamentals. Negotiation is just structured prompt-design in a higher-stakes context.

Best Prompt for Research: Turn AI Into a Literature Scout

The 4-part prompt that turns vague questions into sourced notes with gaps, counterpoints, and next searches.

Why most "research prompts" fail

If you've ever typed research the impact of remote work on productivity into an AI assistant and gotten a confident, well-structured essay with invented statistics, you've hit the single most common failure mode in this category. The model isn't being lazy — it's doing exactly what vague instructions allow. A prompt without a scope, a time window, a source policy, or an output shape will produce fluent text that looks like research but has no factual backbone.

The fix is not a cleverer sentence. The fix is a best prompt for research that is structured enough to constrain hallucination, flexible enough to adapt to any topic, and explicit enough that the model knows what "done" looks like. The four-part template below is the version I keep coming back to after running dozens of literature reviews for clients, students, and product teams.

The 4-part research prompt template

Every research prompt that produces reliable output has the same four moves: Role, Scope, Sources, and Output. Skip any one and quality collapses. Stack all four and even a frontier model behaves like a methodical research assistant instead of a confident storyteller.

Part 1: Role — anchor the model in a perspective

Generic assistants produce generic answers. Researchers produce specific ones. The role line should be a single sentence that names a perspective, a domain, and a stance. "You are a graduate research assistant in organizational psychology, working on a literature review for a thesis chapter." That sentence does more work than it looks like: it activates domain vocabulary, narrows the model toward peer-reviewed conventions, and gives it permission to flag uncertainty in academic terms ("the evidence is mixed," "small-N studies suggest," "methodological caveats apply").

Part 2: Scope — bound the question

The scope line is where most people under-spend. A research question without boundaries will be answered at the model's default scale (usually the whole internet, 1990–2024, no methodology). Force the boundaries: "Focus on peer-reviewed empirical studies from 2018–2025, with at least 30% from the last 24 months. Prioritize meta-analyses and systematic reviews; flag opinion pieces as such." The numbers matter. A model that sees "2018–2025" will not cite a 2010 paper as if it were current; a model that sees "meta-analyses first" will not lead with a single provocative case study.

Part 3: Sources — force a citation policy

This is the part that prevents hallucinated citations. A useful instruction is: "For every factual claim, cite a specific author, year, and venue. If you cannot verify a claim from a source you are confident about, say 'unverified' rather than inventing a reference. Prefer primary sources; use secondary sources only to summarize the primary literature." The word "unverified" is doing a lot of work — it gives the model a sanctioned way out of fabrication, which dramatically reduces confident nonsense. This pattern is consistent with what major model providers recommend in their prompt engineering guides, where grounding and explicit source handling are treated as the single most effective hallucination reducer.

Part 4: Output — define the shape of "done"

The final part is a literal shape: how many sections, what each section contains, and what the final deliverable looks like. "Produce a 1,200-word synthesis with four sections: (1) the dominant finding, (2) two key counterpoints or conflicting results, (3) methodological caveats across the studies, (4) gaps and suggested next searches. End with a bulleted list of 5 specific follow-up queries I should run."

ROLE
You are a graduate research assistant in [DOMAIN], working on
a literature review for a [PROJECT TYPE: thesis chapter / market
analysis / due-diligence report / competitive scan].

SCOPE
Focus on [SOURCE TYPE: peer-reviewed / industry reports / official
docs / a mix] from [YEAR RANGE], with at least 30% from the
last 24 months. Prioritize [PRIORITY: meta-analyses / primary
research / official guidance]; flag [LOWER-TIER] as such.

SOURCES
For every factual claim, cite a specific author, year, and venue.
If you cannot verify a claim, say "unverified" rather than inventing
a reference. Prefer primary sources; use secondary sources only
to summarize the primary literature. Surface conflicting findings
explicitly rather than averaging them away.

OUTPUT
Produce a [WORD COUNT]-word synthesis with sections:
1. The dominant finding
2. Two key counterpoints or conflicting results
3. Methodological caveats across the studies
4. Gaps and suggested next searches
End with a bulleted list of [N] specific follow-up queries.

Three worked examples

The template is the same every time. What changes is the bracketed content. Here are three uses I've shipped in the last month, lightly compressed.

Example 1: Academic literature review

Topic: Does remote work increase or decrease individual productivity? With the role set to organizational psychology PhD candidate, the scope to 2018–2025 peer-reviewed empirical studies with at least 30% from the last 24 months, prioritizing meta-analyses and large-N field studies, the source policy as the template above, and the output set to 1,200 words, four sections, end with 5 follow-up queries — the result was a synthesis that opened with the dominant finding (productivity is stable on average, but distributional: high for some roles, low for collaborative ones), then explicitly surfaced the summarization problem in the underlying studies (heterogeneous outcome measures), and ended with five narrow follow-up queries like "remote work productivity 2024 RCT software engineering" instead of a vague "more research needed."

Example 2: Competitive scan for a product team

Topic: What are the top three AI note-taking apps and how do they differ on retrieval and privacy? Same four-part template, but role set to product manager at a privacy-focused SaaS company, scope to 2024–2025 product documentation, official blogs, and reputable tech press; prioritize official sources over reviews, source policy adjusted to cite a specific URL for every product claim; if a claim cannot be sourced, label it "marketing claim, unverified", output set to 800 words, comparison table at the end, three follow-up queries. The model produced a table I could paste into a Notion page with one light edit.

Example 3: Due-diligence summary

Topic: Summarize the regulatory landscape for AI in healthcare across the US, EU, and UK. Role: policy analyst at a digital health startup preparing for a board meeting. Scope: 2023–2025 official regulatory guidance and legislation; prioritize primary regulatory text over commentary. Sources: cite the specific regulation, agency, and section; if you cannot find a primary source, say "no primary source located". Output: 1,500 words organized by jurisdiction, ending with a risk-rated bullet list and 5 follow-up queries.

You are a policy analyst at a digital health startup preparing
for a board meeting.

Focus on 2023-2025 official regulatory guidance and legislation
across the US, EU, and UK. Prioritize primary regulatory text
(acts, agency guidance, official FAQs) over secondary commentary.
At least 40% of sources should be from the last 12 months.

For every factual claim, cite the specific regulation, agency,
and section. If you cannot find a primary source, say "no primary
source located" rather than inferring. Where US, EU, and UK
positions diverge, present each side separately rather than
smoothing them together.

Produce a 1,500-word synthesis organized by jurisdiction
(US / EU / UK), each section covering: (a) the binding rules,
(b) pending proposals, (c) enforcement record so far. End with
a risk-rated bullet list (High/Med/Low) for a Series B health
AI company, and 5 specific follow-up queries.

Common mistakes that break research prompts

Even with a good template, three mistakes come up over and over. Knowing them up front saves a full re-run.

1. Vague scope. "Research X" is a writing prompt, not a research prompt. Always specify a year range, a source type, and a priority order. If you don't, the model will average across the entire internet, and you'll get a tourist's view of the topic.

2. No source policy. If you don't say what to do when the model can't verify a claim, it will invent a citation. The single word "unverified" as a sanctioned output is the cheapest hallucination reducer you can add. For more on building prompts that produce grounded, verifiable output, see the prompt engineering guide.

3. Output shape that doesn't match the use case. Asking for "a summary" of a 200-paper literature is asking the model to do your job. Specify word count, section count, and the format of the closing deliverable (table, bullet list, follow-up queries). A model that knows it's writing four sections of 300 words behaves very differently from one told to "give me a summary."

How to adapt the template to a new domain

The four parts stay the same; the content under each changes by domain. For academic work, lean hard on the sources part — that's where the standard lives. For market research, lean hard on the output part — tables and comparison matrices are worth more than essays. For policy and regulation, lean hard on the scope part — date windows matter because the field moves in months, not years.

One useful trick: write the output section first. Before you compose the prompt, write down what you want the final deliverable to look like. If you can't describe it concretely, the model can't produce it. The output section is the contract; the role, scope, and sources exist to make it possible to honor that contract.

Quick checklist before you send

Run through this before every research prompt:

  • Role — Is there a specific domain, perspective, and project type named in one sentence?
  • Scope — Are the year range, source types, and priority order explicit?
  • Sources — Is there an explicit citation policy and a sanctioned "unverified" fallback?
  • Output — Is the word count, section count, and closing format specified?

If all four are yes, the prompt will do real work. If any one is missing, expect tourist-grade output. This is also the underlying logic behind the broader pattern of giving the model explicit constraints rather than open-ended instructions — the more specific the brief, the more useful the response.

Best Prompt for Customer Support: Faster Replies, Happier Customers

5 prompt templates for customer support replies that de-escalate, solve, and turn complaints into loyalty.

📅 July 4, 2026 📂 Templates ⏱️ 7 min read

Most best prompt for customer support advice you'll find online is generic. "Be polite. Be clear. Use empathy." That kind of guidance gets you a template that sounds like every other chatbot reply in a customer's inbox — and customers spot it instantly. Real support work is messier: billing disputes, refunds, angry escalations, refunds denied, refund granted, three back-and-forth emails that should have been one. The prompts in this guide are written for those moments, not for the polite FAQ case.

AI is already doing most of this work. A Stanford–MIT field experiment of 5,179 support agents found that access to a generative-AI assistant raised agent productivity by about 14%, with the largest gains for newer agents. In production, the AI customer-service market is on track to grow from $13 billion in 2024 to roughly $84 billion by 2033, and 80% of routine inquiries are now handled by AI before a human sees them. The bottleneck is no longer answer speed — it's tone, accuracy, and knowing when to hand off. That is exactly what a well-written support prompt controls.

1. What makes a customer support prompt actually work

A good support prompt has four ingredients, and skipping any one of them produces replies that feel robotic, evasive, or both:

  • Role and brand voice. Tell the AI who it is — a calm senior support agent for a B2B SaaS company is a different writer than a friendly D2C brand rep. Without a role, the model defaults to generic "helpful assistant" tone that customers have learned to ignore.
  • Customer context. Order number, plan tier, prior interactions, region, language. A reply that ignores context is a reply that asks the customer to repeat themselves — the fastest way to lose trust.
  • Resolution path. Not just "solve the problem" but a clear set of allowed actions (refund up to $50 without approval, escalate billing disputes over $200, etc.). Without bounds, AI invents policies.
  • Empathy guardrails. Explicit instructions on tone for angry, confused, or escalated customers — and an instruction to never mirror a hostile tone back.

Skipping any one of these is the single most common mistake — and it's almost always the empathy guardrails that get dropped, because they feel redundant. They're not. Anthropic's prompt-engineering guidance explicitly calls out that AI trained on patterns can mirror a frustrated customer's tone and end up sounding defensive. The fix is to spell out the tone the AI should keep regardless of what the customer sends.

2. The base system prompt

Use this as the foundation. Paste it into your AI support tool's system prompt, then layer the templates from the next section on top for each ticket type.

You are a senior customer support agent for {BRAND}, a {INDUSTRY} company.
Your replies are concise, warm, and action-oriented. You never argue with a
customer, never invent policies, and never promise a refund or escalation
without qualifying it.

TONE RULES:
- Acknowledge the customer's emotion in the first sentence when they are
  frustrated, confused, or angry. A simple "I understand how frustrating
  that is" is enough; do not over-apologize or use hollow phrases.
- Do not mirror hostile, sarcastic, or dismissive tone, even if the
  customer's message uses those tones. Stay calm and professional.
- Keep replies under 180 words unless the customer explicitly asks for
  detail or the issue requires a step-by-step resolution.
- Use the customer's name once if known. Avoid filler words ("absolutely",
  "certainly", "I'd be happy to") more than once per reply.

RESOLUTION RULES:
- Pull context (order ID, plan, prior tickets) before answering.
- If you do not know the answer, say so plainly and offer to escalate.
- Never fabricate shipping dates, refund timelines, or policy text.
- Allowed actions without approval: {LIST — e.g. refunds under $50,
  account reactivation, password reset links}.
- Escalate to a human when: {TRIGGERS — e.g. refund > $200, legal threat,
  account deletion request, repeated unresolved ticket}.

OUTPUT FORMAT:
- Greeting on its own line is optional; skip it for short replies.
- One short paragraph for acknowledgment.
- Numbered or bulleted steps for any multi-action resolution.
- Closing line: next step or expected wait time. Never "Is there anything
  else I can help with?" as a standalone closer — it feels like a script.

The "Allowed actions without approval" line is what separates a working prompt from a liability. If you don't tell the AI what it can do, it will improvise — and improvising on a refund policy is how you end up with a $500 credit your CFO didn't approve.

3. Five prompt templates for the tickets that actually happen

These are the prompts I send Claude (or any chat model with a system prompt + user input) for the five situations that make up the bulk of real support volume. Adapt the placeholders — they are the only parts you need to change per ticket.

Template A — First reply to a new ticket

SYSTEM: Use the base system prompt above.

USER:
Customer name: {name}
Channel: {email | chat | WhatsApp}
Order/Account ID: {id}
First message: {paste customer's verbatim message}

Write the first reply. Goal: acknowledge within the first sentence,
state the next step, and set an expectation for response time. If the
issue requires investigation, say so explicitly — do not pretend to
have the answer.

Why this works: it forces the AI to quote the customer's exact problem rather than paraphrasing it away, which is the difference between "I understand you have a billing question" (useless) and "I see the duplicate charge on your May 12 invoice — let me pull that up" (useful).

Template B — Handling a billing dispute or refund request

SYSTEM: Use the base system prompt above. Refunds up to $50 can be
issued without approval. Anything larger requires the following
escalation note.

USER:
Customer: {name}
Disputed amount: {amount}
Reason given: {reason}
Order history: {paste 2-3 lines — recent orders, prior refunds}

Task: (1) Acknowledge the dispute without arguing the merits in the
first reply. (2) State what you can confirm from the order history.
(3) If amount <= $50, offer the refund with an ETA of 3-5 business
days. (4) If amount > $50, write an escalation note for a human
agent including: customer ID, dispute amount, order history summary,
recommended action, and any precedent (e.g. "we refunded a similar
dispute on April 8").

This is the template that has saved my team the most time, because it splits the work cleanly: the AI handles the acknowledgment (which is 70% of the reply's emotional weight) and the data prep, while a human only steps in for the actual decision.

Template C — De-escalating an angry or hostile customer

SYSTEM: Use the base system prompt above. For this ticket, do not
attempt to resolve the issue in the first reply. Goal: lower the
emotional temperature and acknowledge what went wrong.

USER:
Customer: {name}
Verbatim message: {paste — do not edit, even if it contains insults}
Prior ticket count from this customer: {n}

Write a reply that: (1) opens with a sincere acknowledgment of what
went wrong from the customer's perspective, not yours. (2) Names the
specific failure (delayed shipping, missed callback, wrong item) if
identifiable from the message. (3) States one concrete action you
will take in the next 24 hours. (4) Avoids defensive language,
discounts, or "unfortunately." (5) Does not ask the customer to
"calm down" or repeat their issue.

The "verbatim message — do not edit" instruction matters. When the AI paraphrases an angry message, it sometimes accidentally softens it, which feels dismissive. Quoting it back signals that the message was actually read.

Template D — Proactive outreach (shipping delay, outage, policy change)

SYSTEM: Use the base system prompt above.

USER:
Reason for outreach: {e.g. "shipping delay of 5+ days on orders
placed after June 1"}
Affected segment: {e.g. "all customers with orders #12000-#12500,
US only"}
Desired response: {e.g. "proactive email + 15% credit on next order"}

Draft the message. Constraints: lead with the apology, not the
credit. Explain the cause in one sentence without blaming a third
party ("supplier delay" is fine; "our warehouse screwed up" is
not). State the credit amount and how it will appear. Include a
single click-to-reply line for customers who want to talk to a
human. No marketing language, no upsell.

Proactive messages are the highest-leverage thing you can ship with AI support. A well-timed, well-worded "we missed the deadline, here's what's happening, here's what we're doing about it" email is worth ten apologies delivered after the customer complains.

Template E — Internal summary for handoff to a human agent

SYSTEM: You are summarizing a support ticket for a human agent. Be
factual, do not editorialize, do not include speculation about the
customer's intent.

USER:
Full ticket transcript: {paste the entire thread}

Produce a structured summary with these sections: (1) Customer
profile: name, account tier, lifetime spend if available. (2) Issue
in one sentence. (3) What has been tried so far. (4) Customer's
stated desired outcome. (5) Recommended next action with rationale.
(6) Risk flags (legal threat, churn risk, repeat complainant). Keep
total length under 250 words.

Use this whenever a ticket crosses any of your escalation triggers. Handing a human agent a 2,000-word transcript and saying "good luck" is a waste of their time; handing them a 200-word structured summary is a gift.

4. The mistakes that ruin otherwise good prompts

After running thousands of AI-generated replies, these are the failure modes that come up again and again. Every one of them is fixable with a small prompt edit — none of them requires retraining the model.

Mistake 1: No escalation triggers

An AI that is never told when to escalate will escalate nothing, or will escalate everything. Both are bad. Specify exact triggers — refund amount thresholds, legal language, account deletion, three failed contacts — and the AI will route correctly.

Mistake 2: Vague tone instructions

"Be friendly and professional" means different things to different models. Replace it with concrete examples: "We never use the word 'unfortunately.' We open with the customer's problem, not our apology. We avoid 'no problem' as a closer — it implies there was a problem." Concrete rules beat abstract adjectives.

Mistake 3: Asking the AI to apologize on behalf of the company

AI apologies are easy to generate and almost always hollow. If your company made a mistake, a human should sign off on the apology text. Use the AI to draft, not to ship.

Mistake 4: Forgetting the customer context window

If your AI does not pull order history, plan, prior tickets, or region before replying, every reply starts from scratch. The customer has to re-explain everything. Most modern support tools let you inject this into the user prompt — do that, and watch your CSAT scores move.

Mistake 5: Letting the AI close the ticket

AI should not have permission to mark a ticket "resolved." It can draft the closing reply and flag the ticket for human review. The human clicks the button. This is one of the simplest governance rules and it eliminates a whole class of "AI closed my ticket without actually fixing it" complaints.

5. Putting it all together

Start with the base system prompt in section 2, even if you only handle one type of ticket. Then add the templates from section 3 as you encounter each ticket type — billing, anger, proactive outreach, handoffs. Finally, borrow the mistake list in section 4 as your QA rubric: every reply your AI generates should be checked against those five failure modes before it goes out.

The point of a best prompt for customer support is not to make the AI sound human. It is to make the AI useful, fast, and consistent enough that the human team can spend their time on the cases that actually require a human. Done well, your agents stop answering the same five questions a hundred times a day and start handling the ten cases a week that actually need their judgment. That is the real metric — not response time, not deflection rate, not even CSAT on its own, but whether the people on your team are doing the work only they can do.

Related: Best Prompt for Email Writing — pair support replies with sharper outbound emails using the same role-and-context pattern.
Related: Best Prompt for Product Description — same structure-and-tone principles, applied to product copy.
Related: 9 Tips for Claude Prompts — the broader Claude prompt-engineering playbook these support templates are built on.

Best Prompt for Tutoring: Learn Anything in 30 Days

The 4-part prompt that turns an LLM into a 30-day personal tutor for any subject with spaced repetition and quizzes.

📅 July 6, 2026 📂 Tutorial ⏱️ 9 min read
#tutoring prompt #AI tutor #spaced repetition #Feynman technique #learning prompt

Why most people get AI tutoring wrong (and what to do instead)

If you have ever opened a chat window, typed "explain X to me," and waited for the AI to dump a textbook summary, you have already experienced the most common failure of the best prompt for tutoring: treating the model like an answer machine instead of a coach. A Harvard randomized controlled trial published in June 2025 found that students using an AI tutor achieved 2× greater learning gains than a traditional active-learning classroom, with effect sizes of 0.73 to 1.3 standard deviations — but those gains only show up when the AI is asked to teach, not to tell. A separate field experiment run by Wharton across ten Taipei high schools in March 2026 found that adaptive sequencing of practice problems added the equivalent of 6–9 months of schooling on the final exam. The single biggest variable in both studies was not the model. It was the prompt.

This article gives you the exact 4-part tutoring prompt structure that turns ChatGPT, Claude, or Gemini into a 30-day personal tutor for any subject. It includes spaced repetition, active recall, the Feynman Technique, and a self-test at the end of every session. You will leave with 4 copy-paste templates, an adaptation for kids vs adults vs technical learners, and the 3 mistakes that flatten a tutor into a Google replacement.

What you'll get: a base tutor system prompt, a daily session template, a 30-day curriculum scaffold, a quiz generator, and an "explain-back" template that uses the Feynman Technique. Paste, edit the subject, start learning.

The 4-part structure of the best prompt for tutoring

Every effective tutoring prompt — whether you are learning calculus, Mandarin, or how a startup's cap table works — has the same four components in the same order:

  1. Role — Who the AI is acting as. Not "helpful assistant," but "expert tutor specializing in {subject} who has taught this exact material to {audience} for 10 years."
  2. The learner's state — Current level, prior knowledge, goal, deadline. A tutor who does not know where you are starting cannot pick the right next step.
  3. The pedagogical method — Active recall, spaced repetition, teach-back, worked examples, socratic questioning. Pick one or two. The method IS the prompt.
  4. The session contract — What each session looks like, how long it runs, and how it ends (always with a 3-question self-test, never with the AI dumping a summary).

Most "study with ChatGPT" prompts online give you only #1 and #3. The two missing pieces — the learner's state and the session contract — are why most users feel like they are learning when they are actually skimming.

1. The base tutor system prompt (paste this first)

Set this as your system prompt for the entire tutoring project. Then each session uses one of the templates below as the user message.

You are a personal tutor specializing in {SUBJECT}. You have taught
{SUBJECT} to {AUDIENCE} for over 10 years and you know the 5–8
common misconceptions students hit at every level.

Your job is to teach, not to answer. When the learner asks a
question, you do NOT give the answer. You:
  (a) ask what the learner already knows or assumes about the topic,
  (b) surface the gap in their understanding with one Socratic
      question,
  (c) provide a worked example only after the learner has attempted
      their own answer, and
  (d) end every session with a 3-question recall quiz and a 1-line
      preview of tomorrow's session.

You never use the phrase "great question" or any other filler praise.
You never dump bullet-point summaries. You always teach one idea per
turn. You adapt vocabulary to the learner's stated level.

Pedagogical methods you are allowed to use:
  • Active recall (asking the learner to retrieve before re-reading)
  • Spaced repetition (re-test prior material every 3 sessions)
  • Feynman technique (learner explains in plain language, you grade)
  • Interleaving (mix topics within a session, do not block-practice)
  • Worked-example fading (full worked → partial worked → blank)

Forbidden behaviors:
  • Giving the answer before the learner has attempted
  • Producing a full chapter summary on demand
  • Praising the learner as a substitute for teaching
  • Mirroring frustration or impatience back to the learner

Once this is in the system prompt slot, every session gets the same "DNA" — the AI will teach instead of answer, regardless of how the user phrases their follow-up. That is what the prompt is doing: codifying a teaching style, not generating answers.

2. The daily session template (the user prompt)

For each session, send this template as your message. Fill in the placeholders. The session will unfold as a back-and-forth, not as a single dump.

SUBJECT: {e.g. "Macroeconomics 101" or "Conversational Japanese"}
CURRENT LEVEL: {e.g. "Complete beginner" or "B2, can read newspaper
articles but struggles with native-speed podcasts"}
WHAT I ALREADY KNOW: {2–4 sentences on your prior exposure}

TODAY'S GOAL: {one sentence — e.g. "Understand how fractional
reserve banking creates money"}

TIME BUDGET: {e.g. "20 minutes"}

Please run this session using these constraints:
  1. Ask me one diagnostic question first to gauge my actual level
     (do not trust the 'level' I self-reported).
  2. Teach the goal in 2–3 micro-lessons, each ending with a
     1-paragraph teach-back where I explain it back to you.
  3. After each of my teach-backs, grade me out of 5 and tell me
     exactly what I missed.
  4. End the session with a 3-question recall quiz (no peeking
     back) and tell me when I should re-test this material.

This template works because it forces the AI into a known structure. Without it, most sessions devolve into "tell me about X" → 8-paragraph answer → "tell me more" → loop. With it, the AI has a checklist to walk through, and you have a runnable script.

Tip: keep a session log. After every tutorial session, paste the AI's final summary into a single document called "{subject}-sessions.md." Re-pasting the prior 2–3 sessions at the start of each new session is the cheapest possible spaced-repetition setup. See our productivity workflow guide for the wider pattern.

3. The 30-day curriculum scaffold (scaling up)

The single-session template scales to a multi-week course with one extra step: ask the AI to design the syllabus in prerequisite order, not in topic order, before day 1. This is the difference between "I learned a bunch of stuff" and "I can solve the problems I needed to solve."

Build me a 30-day curriculum for {SUBJECT} that takes a learner
from {STARTING LEVEL} to {GOAL}. Constraints:

  - Each daily session is 20 minutes.
  - Every 3rd day is a "spaced repetition" review session — it
    re-tests material from days 1, 4, 7, 10, 13, 16, 19, 22, 25, 28.
  - Every 7th day is a full mock test (15 questions, mixed topics).
  - On day 30, I will teach the entire subject back to you using
    the Feynman technique. You will grade me.

Output a table with columns: Day | Topic | Pre-reqs | New
concepts | Review from. Then start Day 1 immediately using the
session template.

That last line is critical: Then start Day 1 immediately using the session template. Without it, the AI will generate the table and stop. With it, the AI keeps moving.

4. The Feynman teach-back prompt (for when you get stuck)

When you feel like you "kind of understand" something but cannot explain it, paste this. It is the highest-leverage single prompt you can use.

I am going to teach you {CONCEPT} as if I were explaining it to a
smart 12-year-old with no background in the subject.

Do NOT interrupt. At the end of my explanation, do the following:
  1. List every factual claim I made that is wrong or imprecise,
     with the correct version.
  2. List any concept I skipped that I clearly need to know.
  3. Rewrite my explanation in simpler language than I used.
  4. Give me a single concrete analogy you would use instead of
     any I used.

Begin by inviting me to teach.

Use this on every weekend of a 30-day course. Most learners are shocked the first time: they think they understand, try to teach, and discover they can only describe the surface. That gap is the actual learning.

5. Adapting the prompt: kids, adults, technical learners

The base system prompt works for adults. Two adjustments matter:

  • For children (ages 8–14): replace "Socratic question" with "question with three concrete answer choices." Replace the 3-question recall quiz with "draw a picture of what you learned and describe it to me." Add a hard rule: "if the learner shows frustration, switch to a story-based example for 5 minutes before resuming."
  • For technical learners (engineers, scientists): add: "For any quantitative claim, write out the equation or pseudocode before explaining. Use real datasets when available. When teaching an algorithm, include its complexity class and the failure case, not just the happy path."

The adaptation you do NOT need: a different model. The same prompt works in ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini, and even local models. The pedagogy lives in the prompt, not in the model weight.

6. Mistakes that flatten a tutor prompt into a search engine

These are the 3 failures I see every week. All three are prompt bugs, not model bugs.

Mistake 1: "Teach me about X" with no session contract

Every prompt without an explicit end-of-session check is a Google replacement, not a tutor. The AI will happily produce 8 paragraphs and call it teaching. Add the 3-question recall quiz, always, even if you delete the rest of the contract.

Mistake 2: Asking the AI to "explain simply" without forbidding simplification

"Explain simply" is a license to drop nuance. A real tutor simplifies selectively, keeps the precise term on second mention, and never sacrifices accuracy for accessibility. Replace the line with: "Simplify selectively. Use plain language on first mention, then introduce the precise term on second mention. Never drop a definition that affects whether the learner can answer a follow-up question."

Mistake 3: Letting the learner (you) skip the teach-back

Most users close the chat after the AI's explanation. The whole learning gain lives in the teach-back step. If a session ends without you typing at least 3 sentences explaining the topic back, the session did not happen. Calendar-block yourself for the teach-back if you have to.

7. A worked example: learning "how transformers work" in 30 days

To make this concrete, here is a worked example of the prompt in action. Subject: "How transformers work (the deep-learning architecture, not the robots)." Starting level: "I have used ChatGPT for a year, I can read Python, I do not know what an attention mechanism is." Time budget: 20 minutes/day for 30 days.

The AI's Day 1 response, trimmed for length:

Day 1: Tokens, not words.
Before we begin, tell me what you already know about how a sentence becomes input to a neural network. I will use that as a starting point. If you say "I have no idea," we start with "a sentence is a string of characters" and work up; if you say "I think it's word embeddings," we skip ahead.

[Then the AI walks you through tokens, embeddings, and the self-attention intuition in 3 micro-lessons, ending with the Feynman teach-back and 3-question recall quiz.]

By Day 7 you have learned positional encoding. By Day 15, multi-head attention. By Day 28, you are running a small transformer on Colab and walking through the code with the AI as tutor. By Day 30, you teach it back in plain language and grade yourself on what you missed.

Try it tonight: pick one subject you have wanted to learn for 6+ months. Paste the base system prompt and the Day 1 session template. Spend 20 minutes. If you cannot describe the day's topic in 3 sentences at the end, the prompt is doing its job — you have found a real gap in your understanding.

8. Stacking this with your other learning workflows

The tutoring prompt slots next to three other PromptLab templates you may already use. Treat the 30-day curriculum as the spine and use these as the muscles:

  • Summarization prompts — for the weekly mock test review. After each mock test, paste the questions you missed and ask the AI to write a 1-page targeted micro-lesson on exactly those gaps. This is the single biggest predictor of long-term retention.
  • 9 tips to write a Claude prompt that actually works — most of those rules (give role, give constraints, show examples, force a format) are baked into the tutor system prompt, but if you find the AI slipping into answer-dumper mode, the 9-tips post is the fastest reset.
  • Claude vs GPT-5 vs Gemini prompting — model-specific tweaks. The base prompt works on all three, but the teach-back step is sharper on Claude 4 and the recall-quiz structure is best on GPT-5. The comparison post has the side-by-side output.
Related: 9 Tips to Write a Claude Prompt That Actually Works · Best Prompt for Summarization: Get the Gist in 2 Sentences · Claude vs GPT-5 vs Gemini: Which Writes the Best Prompt?

Best Prompt for Interview Prep: Practice Answers That Sound Like You

5 prompt templates for mock interviews, STAR stories, and company-specific Q&A without generic fluff.

Why most interview-prep prompts backfire

Type help me prepare for a job interview into any AI assistant and you'll get a wall of generic advice: research the company, use the STAR method, prepare questions to ask. All true, all useless — because you already knew that, and none of it rehearses you. The reason is simple: a vague prompt has nothing to anchor to, so the model defaults to the average of every career blog it ever read.

The fix is a best prompt for interview prep that feeds the model three things it cannot guess: the exact role and company, your real background, and the specific format you want to practice in. Get those three right and the same model that produced platitudes will run a convincing mock interview, rewrite your rambling story into a tight STAR answer, and pressure-test your weakest talking point. Below are five templates that do exactly that, plus the three mistakes that keep people stuck with fluff.

The 4-part interview-prep framework

Every prompt in this guide follows the same skeleton: Role, Context, Task, and Output. Name the interviewer's perspective, load your real material, state the drill, and define what a finished answer looks like. Skip any one part and quality collapses back to generic. This is the same structured-prompt logic behind our broader prompt engineering guide — the more specific the brief, the more useful the response.

Part 1: Role — make the model an interviewer, not a coach

A coach explains; an interviewer interrogates. You want the second. Open with a sentence that assigns a sharp perspective: "You are a senior engineering manager at a Series B fintech, interviewing me for a backend role. You are friendly but rigorous, and you follow up on vague answers instead of letting them slide." That single line changes everything — the model now asks probing follow-ups instead of handing you a checklist.

Part 2: Context — feed it your real material

This is where most people under-invest. Paste the actual job description, three or four bullets from your resume, and one sentence on what worries you. The model can only rehearse the interview you're actually walking into if it knows the role, the level, and your genuine background. Generic in, generic out.

Part 3: Task — name the exact drill

Be specific about the exercise: a full mock interview, a single behavioral question, a STAR rewrite, or a stress-test of one weak answer. "Help me prepare" is not a task. "Ask me one behavioral question at a time, wait for my answer, then critique it before moving on" is.

Part 4: Output — define what "done" looks like

Tell the model the shape of a good answer: how long, what structure, what to flag. "After each of my answers, score it 1–5 on structure, specificity, and relevance, then give me one concrete rewrite suggestion." Without an output contract, you get praise; with one, you get coaching.

Template 1: The full mock interview

This is the workhorse. It runs a realistic back-and-forth where the model plays the interviewer and pushes on soft answers.

ROLE
You are a [SENIORITY] [ROLE TITLE] at [COMPANY / TYPE OF COMPANY],
interviewing me for [POSITION]. You are professional and warm but
rigorous — you ask follow-up questions when an answer is vague.

CONTEXT
Here is the job description: [PASTE JD].
Here is my background: [3-4 RESUME BULLETS].
My biggest worry going in: [ONE SENTENCE].

TASK
Run a mock interview. Ask ONE question at a time, wait for my
answer, then ask a natural follow-up before moving to the next
question. Mix behavioral and role-specific questions.

OUTPUT
After every answer, before your follow-up, give me a one-line
critique (what worked, what to tighten). Keep the interview
flowing like a real conversation.

The "one question at a time" instruction is the whole trick. Without it, the model dumps ten questions at once and you get an exam, not a rehearsal.

Template 2: The STAR story builder

Behavioral questions live or die on structure. The STAR method — Situation, Task, Action, Result — turns a rambling anecdote into a crisp answer. Use this prompt to convert a messy memory into a story that lands.

ROLE
You are an interview coach who specializes in the STAR method.

CONTEXT
The question I need to answer: "[BEHAVIORAL QUESTION]."
Here's the rough story from my experience: [BRAIN-DUMP THE
MESSY VERSION — don't polish it].

TASK
Rewrite this as a tight STAR answer: Situation, Task, Action,
Result. Keep it under 90 seconds spoken (~200 words). Make the
Result quantified if the numbers exist; if they don't, tell me
exactly what metric I should dig up.

OUTPUT
Return the STAR answer, then a bulleted list of the follow-up
questions an interviewer is most likely to ask about it.

The last line is underrated: knowing the likely follow-ups is half the preparation. If you're prepping stories that highlight negotiation or leadership wins, the same structure pairs well with our negotiation prompt templates for framing high-stakes conversations.

Template 3: Company-specific question prediction

Different companies interview differently. A prompt that predicts the actual questions — grounded in the job description and the company's stated values — beats a generic list every time.

ROLE
You are a hiring insider who has seen many interview loops at
companies like [COMPANY / INDUSTRY].

CONTEXT
Job description: [PASTE JD].
Company values / mission (if known): [PASTE OR SUMMARIZE].
The team / product this role supports: [ONE LINE].

TASK
Predict the 10 most likely interview questions for this specific
role — a mix of behavioral, technical/role-specific, and
culture-fit. For each, note in one line WHY they'd ask it.

OUTPUT
Group the questions by category. Flag the 3 that are hardest
to answer well so I can prep those first.

Template 4: The weak-answer stress test

You already know your shakiest answer — the salary question, the gap in your resume, the "why are you leaving." Point the model straight at it.

ROLE
You are a tough but fair interviewer.

CONTEXT
Here is my current answer to "[THE QUESTION YOU DREAD]":
[YOUR DRAFT ANSWER].

TASK
Poke holes in this answer the way a skeptical interviewer would.
Then rewrite it so it's honest, confident, and doesn't invite
red-flag follow-ups.

OUTPUT
Return: (1) the 3 weakest points in my current answer, (2) a
rewritten version, (3) the one follow-up question I should be
ready for.

Template 5: The reverse interview (questions to ask them)

Strong candidates ask sharp questions. This prompt generates ones tailored to the role instead of the tired "what's the culture like?"

ROLE
You are a career strategist.

CONTEXT
Role: [POSITION] at [COMPANY].
What I actually care about: [GROWTH / STABILITY / TECH STACK /
MANAGEMENT STYLE — pick 2].

TASK
Give me 8 smart questions to ask the interviewer that (a) signal
I understand the role, and (b) surface information I genuinely
need to decide. No generic "what's a typical day like."

OUTPUT
Sort into: questions for the hiring manager, and questions for
the team/peers. One line each on what a good answer sounds like.

3 mistakes that keep your prep generic

1. Not pasting the real job description. The single biggest quality lever is context. A model prepping you for "a marketing role" and one prepping you for the exact JD you're applying to produce completely different mock interviews. Paste the real thing.

2. Letting the model dump every question at once. A mock interview is a conversation, not a quiz. If you don't force "one question at a time, wait for my answer," you lose the follow-up pressure that makes rehearsal realistic — and follow-ups are where real interviews get hard.

3. Accepting praise instead of demanding critique. AI assistants default to being encouraging. Unless your output contract explicitly asks for scores, weak points, and rewrites, you'll get a cheerleader. Prep needs a critic. Always build the critique into the prompt.

How to string the templates together

Run them in sequence for a full prep session: start with Template 3 to predict the questions, use Template 2 to build STAR stories for the behavioral ones, run Template 4 on your dreaded question, then do a live Template 1 mock the day before. Finish with Template 5 so you walk in with sharp questions of your own. The whole loop takes an hour and beats re-reading interview blogs for a week — the same way a tailored resume prompt beats a generic template.

The through-line across all five: the model is only as good as the context you feed it and the output contract you set. Give it your real material, make it an interviewer instead of a coach, and demand critique over comfort. Do that and AI becomes the practice partner you actually needed — one that sounds like your interview, and helps you sound like you.

Related: Best Prompt for Resume: Beat the ATS and Sound Human · Best Prompt for Cover Letter: Templates That Get Read · Best Prompt for Negotiation: Get the Deal Without the Back-and-Forth
