Best Prompt for Contract Review: Spot Risk Clauses Before You Sign

The 5-section prompt that flags liability, termination, IP, and payment terms — with plain-English summaries.

Why "review this contract" is the wrong prompt

summarize this contract or flag any risks in this NDA. The output that comes back is usually a polite paraphrase of the document — neat headings, no surprises, and almost nothing about what would actually bite you if things went wrong. That is because a vague prompt asks the model to be a summarizer, when what you really need is a skeptical counterparty.

A best prompt for contract review does three things the casual prompt skips: it tells the model which role to play (a sharp counterparty lawyer or contracts manager, not a friendly assistant), it names the contract categories you actually care about (liability, termination, IP, payment, indemnification, governing law — pick from a checklist), and it sets a hard output format (plain-English summary per clause, the worst-case reading, and a red/yellow/green flag). Get those right and the same AI that gave you a fluffy summary will surface the auto-renewal trap, the one-sided indemnity, and the payment term that quietly buries your net-30.

The 5-section review framework

Every prompt in this guide follows the same five-section skeleton: Role, Document, Focus Areas, Output Format, and Escalation Rules. Name the perspective, paste the contract, list the clause families you care about, define what a useful answer looks like, and tell the model when to stop and tell you to call a real lawyer. Skip any one and quality collapses back to paraphrase. This mirrors the structured-prompt logic from our broader prompt engineering guide — specifics in, specifics out.

Section 1: Role — make the model a counterparty, not a tutor

The default AI persona is helpful and balanced. For contract review you want the opposite: someone who is paid to find what's wrong with the other side's draft. Open with a sharp persona line: "You are a senior commercial contracts lawyer reviewing an agreement on behalf of the receiving party. You are skeptical, you read fine print, and you flag anything that shifts risk, money, or IP away from me." That single sentence changes the tone of every clause analysis that follows.

Section 2: Document — paste it whole

Paste the full contract text, not a summary. If it's a long agreement, paste it section by section with clear headers; the model performs dramatically better when it sees the actual wording, including definitions and the boilerplate at the back. Include any schedules, exhibits, statements of work, and side letters — risk often hides in the attachments, not the body.

Section 3: Focus areas — name the clause families

"Find the risks" is too vague. Instead, list the families you want reviewed: liability and indemnification caps, termination rights (for cause / for convenience / change of control), IP ownership and license scope, payment terms and late-fee mechanics, confidentiality and residuals, non-compete / non-solicit, warranty disclaimers, limitation of liability, governing law and dispute resolution (arbitration vs. court, venue), assignment, audit rights, and data protection / processing addenda. The model will produce a much sharper analysis when it knows which axes to scan.

Section 4: Output format — define "done"

Without an output contract, you get a flowing essay. With one, you get a review you can actually act on. The right format: per-clause breakdown with plain-English summary, worst-case reading, red/yellow/green flag, and a one-line edit. Add a "Top 5 issues to negotiate" list forces the model to prioritize, which is what you actually need.

Section 5: Escalation rules — know when to stop using AI

The most useful line: "If you encounter regulated activity, personal data subject to GDPR/PDPA, cross-border IP assignment, or anything that could trigger a regulatory filing, do not give a legal answer — instead, flag it as 'ESCALATE TO A LAWYER' and explain why." That protects you from treating AI as a substitute for counsel when stakes are real.

Template 1: General commercial contract review

This is the workhorse for any MSAs, SOWs, vendor agreements, or partnership contracts. Get a prioritized risk review back fast.

ROLE
You are a senior commercial contracts lawyer reviewing an agreement
on behalf of the receiving party. You are skeptical, you read fine
print, and you flag anything that shifts risk, money, or IP away
from me.

DOCUMENT
[Paste the full contract, including schedules, exhibits, SOWs,
and any side letters. If it is very long, paste in clearly labeled
chunks.]

FOCUS AREAS
Review specifically for: liability caps and indemnification
(both directions); termination rights (for cause / for convenience
/ change of control); IP ownership and license scope (including
residuals); payment terms and late-fee mechanics; warranty
disclaimers; limitation of liability; confidentiality and residuals;
non-compete / non-solicit; governing law, venue, and dispute
resolution; assignment and change-of-control; data protection
(DPA, sub-processors, breach notice); audit rights.

OUTPUT FORMAT
For each material clause, give me:
  1. Plain-English summary (2 lines max).
  2. Worst-case reading (what does this clause allow the other
     side to do?).
  3. Flag: RED (must renegotiate) / YELLOW (negotiate if you can)
     / GREEN (acceptable as-is).
  4. Suggested edit (one sentence — what to push back with).
Close with a "Top 5 issues to negotiate, in priority order" list.

ESCALATION
If anything involves regulated activity, personal data under
GDPR/PDPA/HIPAA, cross-border IP assignment, antitrust, or
regulatory filings, flag it as "ESCALATE TO A LAWYER" instead
of answering.

The "worst-case reading" line is the trick. Without it, the model writes what each clause means. With it, the model writes what each clause allows the other side to do if pushed — which is the version that actually matters at the negotiating table.

Template 2: NDA review (the one most people sign without reading)

NDAs look harmless. They aren't. This prompt catches the duration trap, the residuals carve-out, the non-solicit hidden in plain sight, and the "third-party beneficiary" clause that lets affiliates in.

ROLE
You are an NDA reviewer. You have seen hundreds of mutual and
one-way NDAs go wrong.

DOCUMENT
[Paste the NDA.]

FOCUS AREAS
In particular, check: definition of Confidential Information
(does it cover oral disclosures? derivatives? is "marked" required?);
term and survival (how many years? does it survive termination?);
exclusions (what's NOT confidential — public domain, prior
knowledge, independent development, reverse engineering);
permitted disclosures (legal compulsion, affiliates);
non-solicit / non-compete clauses hidden inside;
residuals clause (does it allow the recipient to use anything
in "residual memory"? — usually a RED flag);
assignment and third-party beneficiary;
governing law and remedies (is injunctive relief available
without proof of damages?).

OUTPUT FORMAT
Return a clause-by-clause table with the four columns above
(summary / worst-case / flag / suggested edit). Then a one-paragraph
"is this mutual or one-way, really?" verdict and a Top 3 list of
negotiations if any.

ESCALATION
Same rules as above.

Most NDAs are mutual in name but one-way in practice — usually because the residuals clause or the non-solicit quietly favors the discloser. This prompt surfaces that within a minute, so you can sign quickly when it's fine and push back when it isn't.

Template 3: Service / vendor agreement (where the real money hides)

Vendor and SaaS agreements are where most businesses lose the most money, because the liability cap, SLA credits, and termination-for-convenience clauses are all calibrated against the customer. This prompt checks the three numbers that matter: liability cap as a percentage of fees, SLA credit cap, and notice period for termination for convenience.

ROLE
You are a procurement contracts specialist reviewing a vendor
agreement on behalf of the customer.

DOCUMENT
[Paste the agreement, including any URL-linked terms that the
vendor has incorporated by reference.]

FOCUS AREAS
Pay particular attention to: liability cap (is it 1x fees? less?
uncapped for IP and confidentiality?); indemnification (one-way
or mutual? who indemnifies for what?); service levels and SLA
credits (cap and sole remedy clauses); data ownership and
portability on termination; termination for convenience
(notice period and any early-termination fee); price-change
mechanics and renewal auto-renewal windows; audit rights;
subcontracting and assignment; security and breach notification
(especially if personal data is involved).

OUTPUT FORMAT
Three sections:
  (a) A "Money & Exit" summary — the three numbers and clauses
      that matter most for the total cost of ownership.
  (b) The per-clause breakdown in the same format as Template 1.
  (c) A "before you sign" checklist: the 5 things to ask the
      vendor to change.

ESCALATION
Same rules. Pay extra attention to data protection addenda —
those should always be reviewed by counsel.

Template 4: Employment / contractor agreement

For offer letters, employment contracts, and independent contractor agreements, the risks are different: IP assignment, non-compete enforceability, non-solicit windows, and garden leave. This prompt reads the agreement as if it might end up in court.

ROLE
You are an employment lawyer reviewing an employment or
independent contractor agreement. You know which clauses are
likely unenforceable in my jurisdiction and which are deal-breakers.

DOCUMENT
[Paste the agreement. State the country/state, because
non-compete enforceability varies wildly.]

FOCUS AREAS
Specifically: IP assignment (does it cover prior inventions?
residuals? is there a "prior inventions" schedule?); non-compete
(is it likely enforceable here? what is the duration and scope?);
non-solicit (employees, customers, both? duration?); garden
leave; compensation structure (base, bonus, equity treatment on
termination); termination notice periods and severance;
restrictive covenants that survive termination; governing law.

OUTPUT FORMAT
Per-clause breakdown with the four columns, plus a "red flags I
should not sign without changing" list and a one-paragraph note
on any clause that is likely unenforceable as drafted.

ESCALATION
Employment law is highly local. If you cannot determine
enforceability for the named jurisdiction, say so plainly and
recommend local counsel.

3 mistakes that make AI contract review useless

1. Pasting a summary instead of the full contract. A summary has already filtered out what you wanted found. Always paste the raw document. If the contract is very long, paste in clearly labeled sections and tell the model what came before.

2. Asking for "any risks" without a focus list. "Any risks" produces a balanced essay. A checklist of clause families produces a prioritized review. The latter is what you can act on before signing.

3. Treating the output as legal advice. AI contract review is a triage tool — it finds the clauses worth arguing about and the ones worth walking away over. It is not a substitute for a lawyer on regulated activity, regulated data, IP assignment, or anything that could trigger a regulatory filing. Always include the escalation rule in the prompt, and always escalate when it fires.

How to use these prompts in real life

Run Template 1 (or the more specific variant) before any non-trivial contract goes to counsel — the prompt will surface the 3-5 issues that are worth the lawyer's hourly rate, saving billable time. For NDAs and low-stakes vendor agreements, the prompt alone often suffices. For anything with personal data, regulated activity, or cross-border IP, treat the output as a triage note and route it to a lawyer with the flagged clauses highlighted. The whole loop beats re-reading the contract — the same way a tailored summarization prompt beats a TL;DR.

The through-line across all four templates: the model is only as useful as the role you assign, the document you paste, the focus list you give it, the output format you demand, and the escalation rule you build in. Set those five and AI becomes a fast, skeptical first-pass reviewer — one that catches the auto-renewal trap, one-sided indemnity, and residuals carve-out before you sign.
